#5099. Nomura explores sale of crypto unit Laser Digital: Bloomberg

The potential sale of Laser Digital follows a fourth straight quarterly loss for Nomura’s European operations.

Japanese financial services giant Nomura Holdings is reportedly weighing a sale of its cryptocurrency subsidiary Laser Digital as Nomura’s European operations continue to post losses.

Nomura has held talks with potential buyers, including Marex Group, Bloomberg reported Wednesday. The company has yet to make a final decision and could keep the business, according to the report.

Nomura’s European businesses posted a pretax loss of 7.4 billion yen ($46.5 million) in the quarter ended Sept. 30, 2026, their fourth consecutive quarterly loss. The company posted pretax profits in every other reporting region over the same period. Nomura did not disclose whether Laser Digital, which is headquartered in Switzerland, contributed to the European result.

Nomura is one of Japan’s largest financial services groups, with its investment management division overseeing 152.8 trillion yen ($960 billion) in assets as of September 2026.

The potential sale comes less than three months after Laser Digital Japan secured the country’s first new crypto exchange license in four years, allowing the firm to expand its institutional crypto trading services in Japan.

Established in 2022, Laser Digital provides institutional cryptocurrency trading, asset management and venture investment services. The company has also pursued expansion in the United States, receiving preliminary approval from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency for its proposed national trust bank in May.

During a January earnings call, chief financial officer Hiroyuki Moriuchi said the company would reduce its cryptocurrency trading positions to limit short-term volatility while continuing to expand Laser Digital over the medium to long term.

#5097. Stablecoin supply drops $14.2 billion in largest contraction since 2022 bear market

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

USDT and USDC shed nearly $11 billion in supply over the past month as analysts flag less buying power for a crypto rebound.

Crypto's pool of stablecoins is shrinking at its fastest pace in more than four years, alongside a stalled market recovery

A Wednesday report from Glassnode found that the supply of major stablecoins fell by $14.2 billion over the past 30 days, the biggest drop since the 2022 crypto bear market. For comparison, the 30-day stablecoin supply decline nearly reached $30 billion in June 2022, roughly five months before bitcoin reached its bear market low of $15,500 that November.

The total stablecoin supply now sits just below $280 billion, levels not seen since September 2025.

"A shrinking supply means holders are redeeming stablecoins for dollars and taking that cash out of the market, which leaves less fuel for a rally," Glassnode wrote.

A separate report from CryptoQuant on Tuesday focused on the deteriorating supply of Tether's USDT, the long-time stablecoin market leader. Its market capitalization fell $6.6 billion over the 60 days through Nov. 7, more than twice the $3.2 billion drop over a similar period in December 2022 following the collapse of the FTX exchange.

USDT currently accounts for roughly 62.8% of stablecoin supply, followed by Circle's USDC at 25.1%, according to The Block's data.

Gold-backed tokens have held steady by comparison, with a combined market cap of $5.61 billion. That remains below February's $6.31 billion peak when gold was at an all-time high of around $5,350 per troy ounce.

Glassnode said that a return to stablecoin supply growth would be the "first sign that new money is coming in." Meanwhile, CryptoQuant said a local bitcoin bottom could form before USDT supply flips back to positive territory.

#5095. Morning Minute: What Anthropic’s $1.4 Trillion IPO Means for Crypto

Morning Minute: What Anthropic’s $1.4 Trillion IPO Means for Crypto

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

💵 What Anthropic’s $1.4 Trillion IPO Means for Crypto

Several key new details for Anthropic’s pending IPO were released on Tuesday. The IPO price range was set at $70 to $78 a share, selling 825 million Class A shares to raise about $64.4 billion (at the top of the range). Pricing is expected November 18, with trading starting November 19 on Nasdaq under ANT. Roughly 20% of the deal is set aside for retail, which is about double what a book this size usually gives individual investors.

Notably on Hyperliquid, ANT is trading at $1.8T with $100M in daily trading volume and $200M in open interest (a big enough market to provide some signal). So perhaps traders are a bit more bullish on ANT price action than Wall Street.

Alongside the IPO sits a $26 billion private placement of non-voting Class N stock, bought at the IPO price by seven of Anthropic’s own compute suppliers. Google and Nvidia take about $10 billion each, AMD $2.5 billion, Broadcom, Micron and Microsoft about $1 billion apiece, TSMC $500 million. Those shares carry no lock-up. Combined with the IPO, Anthropic walks away with roughly $84.6 billion for general corporate purposes, mostly compute.

Regarding financials, Anthropic just reported revenue of $33.2 billion through nine months against $2.5 billion a year earlier and a $1.26 billion operating loss. It reported a $104 billion net loss, almost all of it a non-cash charge on convertible notes. And $900.9 billion in committed spending, with $70.7 billion due inside twelve months.

The liquidity drain thesis has been circulating since April, when CoinDesk laid out the case that mega-IPOs pull speculative money from the same pool that funds crypto. Retail allocations are the mechanism. A dollar earmarked for an IPO ticket is a dollar not in Bitcoin or memecoins.

We already ran this experiment. SpaceX listed June 12 at more than $2 trillion. Bitcoin fell 29% going into it, bottomed at $58,500 on June 30, and then rose 37% off that low. But notably, the Bitcoin bottom was effectively in on June 5 at $59k, a full week ahead of the SPCX IPO.

So the question is not whether an IPO pulls money out. It’s whether that already happened. Bitcoin fell roughly 15% from its early-October high through a $6.3 billion exchange hack, an AI capex selloff and a Democratic sweep.

Two things make this deal smaller than it looks for crypto. The retail slice is roughly $12.9 billion against SpaceX’s $22 billion, about 59% as much. And the $26 billion private placement never touches public market liquidity at all, because it comes from Anthropic’s vendors, who then get paid back in compute contracts.

Thus, if the SpaceX pattern repeats, and with the ~60% factor, we could expect BTC to drop another 5% or so into the Anthropic IPO (notably, that’s right where the 200-week moving average is). And we would expect the effective bottom to be in sometime in the next 1-2 days.

The good news is, the pain is mostly behind us, in that scenario. We will find out soon enough if IPO history repeats itself for crypto…

#5092. Sen. Warren, Merkley raise ethics concerns over Trump aide’s prediction market bets

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

In August, the CFTC ordered Gabriel Perez to pay more than $172,000 after he allegedly read Trump's prepared remarks shortly before delivery and placed his bets on Kalshi mention markets.

Democratic Sens. Elizabeth Warren and Jeff Merkley are demanding answers from the White House after its teleprompter operator was accused of using advance information to place bets on President Donald Trump's speeches.

The lawmakers say that the White House has failed to take "meaningful action to address ethics concerns," in a letter to White House Counsel Will Scharf.

"We are writing to request information about how the White House is responding to allegations from earlier this year that a key presidential aide enriched himself by abusing access to nonpublic information, and how this alleged incident is shaped by the White House’s lax ethics and anticorruption policies and practices," they said.

In August, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission ordered Gabriel Perez to pay more than $172,000 after he allegedly read Trump's prepared remarks shortly before delivery and placed his bets on Kalshi mention markets, which try to predict specific words that public figures will use. Perez's situation is one of several in which people have used inside information to place bets on prediction markets, including an active-duty U.S. Army soldier who used confidential information to place bets on Polymarket ahead of former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro’s capture earlier this year.

Warren is set to take on a larger role after Democrats took control of the Senate and House in last week's midterm elections. She will lead the Senate Banking Committee, where she has said she would seek to "end the current Wild West-style lack of oversight in this area, with corrupt insiders profiting." Another Democrat, Rep. Ro Khanna, who is weighing a 2028 presidential bid, called for a ban on sports prediction markets on Tuesday.

In the letter, Warren and Merkley said that the White House needs to do more than send a warning memo and take heed of what other administrations have done, including mandatory training.

"More action is required—from the President, from you, and from other White House officials—to provide the public with confidence in the integrity of the federal government under President Trump," the senators said.

Specifically, Warren and Merkley said they want information on whether other employees had "misappropriated insider knowledge to place any bets, trades, purchase contracts, or taken any similar action for personal gain," and if training is available for nonpublic information.

The White House did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

#5089. DogeOS ‘very, very close’ to launching DeFi features, app layer for Dogecoin: CEO

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Jordan Jefferson says development and audits are complete as the team coordinates builders, wallets and exchanges ahead of launch.

DogeOS is nearing a mainnet launch that will let developers build apps and DeFi features for Dogecoin, founder and CEO Jordan Jefferson told The Block.

"Development is complete on version one; audits are complete," Jefferson said on a Wednesday episode of The Starting Block podcast. "Our main focus right now across the board is getting DogeOS to mainnet, which is very, very close."

The project is building an EVM-compatible app layer for Dogecoin that would give DOGE holders more ways to use the asset aside from just payments and tipping.

"We all love Dogecoin, but there wasn't much to do other than buy it, hold it, send it to your friends and tip people, which is awesome," Jefferson said. "But what if you could do more fun stuff?"

He said this could include games and other ways to earn Doge, but stressed that DeFi infrastructure is necessary if Dogecoin is to become "money for more people."

Jefferson said more than 100 builders have been working on projects and the team is working on supporting those developers and working to integrate with wallets, exchanges and other protocols.

This follows last month's launch of Dogecoin Labs, which brought together developers and products from DogeOS, the Dogecoin Foundation and House of Doge.

"Dogecoin is just too big to count on [donations] forever," he said.

#5088. Uncalled US House races could send more crypto allies to Congress

The remaining races feature pro-crypto candidates and others without a stated position, giving advocacy groups what they see as “an opportunity” for outreach.

Just over a week after a “blue wave” handed Democrats control of the US House and Senate, 14 races have yet to be called. The results so far have added crypto supporters in both chambers and the remaining races could bring more into Congress.

Three candidates in the uncalled races received backing from crypto political action committees (PACs). According to Stand With Crypto, every candidate in those races is either strongly pro-crypto or has no stated position.

As crypto advocacy groups look ahead to 2028, they’re turning their attention to candidates who have yet to take a public stance on crypto.

Crypto PACs backed three candidates in US House races that remain undecided. Two incumbents lead in Alaska and North Carolina, while a Louisiana state senator faces a December runoff.

Rep. Nick Begich leads in Alaska’s US House race. Defend American Jobs, Fairshake’s Republican-facing affiliate, backed him with $508,142 in the August primary. The same PAC put $150,600 behind Rep. David Rouzer, who leads in North Carolina’s 7th District.

Louisiana state Sen. Blake Miguez will face Democrat Chauna Banks in a Dec. 12 runoff for the state’s 6th District. The Fellowship PAC backed Miguez with $250,000.

The Associated Press called the race in South Carolina’s 2nd District for incumbent Rep. Joe Wilson on the morning of Nov. 11. Stand With Crypto rates Wilson as “strongly supportive of crypto.” His win brought the group’s House tally to 246 crypto supporters and 107 opponents, while the Senate stood at 50 supporters and 33 opponents.

In the remaining races, Stand With Crypto lists each candidate as either strongly supportive of crypto or having no stated position. “The new members that have no stance present an opportunity,” Blockchain Association Chief Policy Officer Lindsay Fraser told Cointelegraph.

Nearly one in four members of the incoming Congress will be new, Fraser said, and many have given little thought to blockchain technology. “To us, that represents a blank slate in terms of education,” she said.

Blockchain Association is planning a January fly-in to reach newly elected lawmakers. “Leaving the 120th Congress with more members in that A and B category would be a huge success,” she said, referring to Stand With Crypto’s ratings. The group gives an A to candidates who strongly support crypto and a B to those who are somewhat supportive.

Fraser said age plays a bigger role than party affiliation in how lawmakers vote on crypto. She said members under 55 are much more likely to support the industry and that the incoming class skews younger.

Fraser said she wants the industry to enter the 2028 election “with two candidates who […] are thoughtful in regards to the industry,” so the sector can avoid “living and dying by the outcome of that election.”

#5086. Decrypt Becomes First News Publisher to Offer Perps Trading, Powered by Solana's Phoenix

Decrypt Becomes First News Publisher to Offer Perps Trading, Powered by Solana's Phoenix

What happens when the place you read the news becomes the place you act on it?

The news that moves markets and the tools to trade them have long lived in separate places. Decrypt is bringing them together.

Decrypt is integrating perpetual futures trading, powered by Phoenix, a fully onchain perpetuals exchange on Solana, making it the first media platform to offer perps trading directly alongside its journalism.

Readers will be able to take long or short positions on crypto, stocks and commodities from the same place they follow the news that moves them, without switching apps, connecting external wallets or moving funds between platforms.

"Information influences decisions, shapes opinions and moves markets," said Ilan Hazan, co-founder and COO of Decrypt. "A reader might discover an opportunity through a publication, develop a view and then leave to act on it somewhere else. The publisher monetizes the impression, while someone else monetizes the transaction. It means the publisher captures only a small part of the economic activity that its information and distribution generates."

Rather than treating media and financial products as separate businesses, he said, Decrypt is bringing them together. "We're allowing readers to go from understanding what's happening in the markets to taking a position, without leaving Decrypt.”

"Partnering with Decrypt closes the gap between reading the news and acting on it," said Kabir Sethi, Head of Business Development at Phoenix. "With over 100 Phoenix perpetuals markets built directly into Decrypt, readers can take in a story, form a view, and trade it in the same place. We are excited to help play a part in transforming Decrypt into a true information exchange."

Perpetual futures, or perps, are contracts that let traders bet on an asset's price rising or falling without an expiry date, typically using leverage. Leverage magnifies both gains and losses, and positions can be liquidated quickly when markets move against them.

Phoenix offers markets in crypto, U.S. stocks, gold and pre-IPO companies around the clock, with up to 40x leverage and low fees, according to its website. It is developed by Ellipsis Labs, a team that has built exclusively on Solana and whose CEO, Eugene Chen, has described perps as having "crazy product market fit."

"These are very, very strong, high revenue businesses, probably some of the best in the industry," Chen said of perps exchanges in a podcast interview with Blockworks' Lightspeed earlier this year. Phoenix routes each trade at the moment it lands onchain, splitting it between its order book and market makers "based on what's going to give you the best price," he explained.

Perps trading runs through the Decrypt Money Account, a single self-custodial account on Solana designed to work without seed phrases, gas fees or bridging.

One balance now funds it all: prediction markets on Decrypt Predict, Jupiter-powered token swaps, Earn and, now, perpetual futures. Users can also send funds privately, after Decrypt added confidential transfers through Solana infrastructure firm Helius.

Imagine reading about a public company's earnings and taking a position on its stock, at any hour. Or following a breaking story about Bitcoin and going long or short in the same moment.

From information to execution, in one place.

Announced in September, The Information Exchange is Decrypt's vision for the next generation of financial media: a single destination bringing together news, intelligence, market data, probabilities, direct financial participation—with perps forming the latest building block in that infrastructure.

News moves markets. Now readers can move with it.

Perpetual futures are leveraged products and carry a high risk of loss. Availability may vary by region.

#5082. SEC enters fight over who regulates perpetual derivatives on stocks in the US

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The SEC filed an amicus brief, a court document in support of the CFTC after it was sued last week by the Capital Markets Alliance seeking to overturn approval of those products.

The Securities and Exchange Commission is stepping into the brewing fight over whether its sister agency should have approved perpetual derivatives on individual stocks in the U.S.

This week, the SEC filed an amicus brief, a court document in support of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission after it was sued last week by the Capital Markets Alliance seeking to overturn approval of those products. The CMA is an industry association with members including Cboe and Citadel Securities.

The SEC pushed back against the CMA's assertion that the CFTC has "gone rogue." The CFTC's new rule changes are also subject to the SEC's review and the SEC said it collaborates often with the CFTC, which is vital when it comes to overseeing equity perpetuals, the agency said in the brief.

"The SEC’s amicus brief addresses why that depiction—of a CFTC gone rogue, and an SEC gone missing—is mistaken," the agency said.

In its lawsuit, CMA said that the CFTC violated the Administrative Procedure Act — a law that governs how federal agencies propose rules — because the perpetuals derivatives should be under the SEC's jurisdiction. The CMA also argued that the CFTC did not explain its decision, and asked the court to "vacate the approvals."

Since then, a group of crypto firms including Coinbase, Kraken parent Payward, Kalshi, DRW Holdings, Paradigm and Jump Trading have filed a joint amicus brief against CMA.  Three of those firms — Coinbase, Kalshi and Payward — have submitted applications with the CFTC to list equity perps tied to U.S. stocks, and the applications were deemed approved on Nov. 2.

Others have also filed amicus briefs, including economic advocacy organization Better Markets and former SEC Commissioner Allison Herren Lee, who said that the new perpetuals should be regulated by the SEC.

"Although labeled by the CFTC and these trading platforms as ‘perpetual futures,’ these products are in fact securities swaps that are supposed to be regulated by the SEC with strong investor protections," Better Markets said in a statement on Tuesday. "That’s because these types of financial products contributed to the 2008 crash and pose an unreasonably high risk of causing total losses to retail investors."

#5080. OKX-ICE Tokenized Stock Venue Sets Launch, Turning to Crypto Companies After Big Tech Opts Out

OKX-ICE Tokenized Stock Venue Sets Launch, Turning to Crypto Companies After Big Tech Opts Out

After weeks of rejections from corporate America, OKX and ICE’s tokenized stock venue has found its launch lineup: crypto’s own public companies.

OKXICE said Wednesday that its Tokenized Securities Venue will open Nov. 23, letting eligible U.S. customers trade tokenized securities around the clock in the OKX app.

More than 70 assets are expected to be listed, including securities from Coinbase, Circle, Robinhood, Strategy, BitGo, Securitize and Better.com, plus 59 ETFs from Direxion. Strategy will also list its preferred stocks.

That’s a very different roster from the one the venue started with. As of its Nov. 5 notice, 67 companies, including every member of the “Magnificent Seven” tech giants, had objected to having their shares traded on the platform. Coinbase, Robinhood and Strategy were notably absent from that list, and all three are now among the venue’s launch issuers.

“The SEC has created a real opening for American markets to lead the next era of finance,” said former New York Gov. Andrew Cuomo, who co-chairs OKXICE, a joint venture between OKX and NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange.

Securitize will provide the tokenization infrastructure. Each token represents an entitlement backed one-for-one by a real U.S. share, carrying dividends, voting rights and other shareholder benefits, according to the announcement.

Trading will run through automated market maker pools built on Uniswap v4 on X Layer, OKX’s Ethereum layer-2 network, and customers will hold their tokens in self-custodial wallets.

Strategy CEO Phong Le called tokenization on the venue “a natural next step” for the company’s preferred stocks, such as STRC.

Access is limited to eligible U.S.-based OKX customers who activate a CeDeFi Wallet, pass identity verification and complete onboarding with OKXICE. The venue operates under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act failed to advance in the Senate. Under the exemption, companies can keep third-party tokenized versions of their stock off a given venue by objecting within 30 days.

The launch caps a busy stretch for Wall Street tokenization. DTCC’s new tokenization service, which runs partly on the Canton Network, went live in late October, and Morgan Stanley said it was among the first banks to use it.

#5078. Strategy, Metaplanet lose MSCI index membership in November reshuffle

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The index changes take effect Nov. 30, following months of debate over the eligibility of crypto treasury companies.

MSCI is moving to delist bitcoin treasury companies Strategy Strategy, Inc. (MSTR) from its USA Index and Metaplanet (3350.T) from its Japan Small Cap Index at the close of Nov. 30, according to filings published Wednesday.

The removals were mostly expected, especially after MSCI adopted new eligibility rules for so-called non-operating companies last month, which put several crypto treasury firms on the proverbial chopping block. Ethereum treasury company Sharplink was not listed among the deletions in the November small-cap review.

The new rules look at companies' operating assets and five financial measures, replacing temporary restrictions on firms that held at least half their assets in crypto. MSCI previously said that companies on its indexes would face removal if they failed this screening for their two most recent annual filing periods.

Strategy Executive Chairman Michael Saylor and Phong Le previously spoke out against the rule change, calling the proposed treatment of digital asset treasuries "discriminatory." In a September letter, Strategy argued that exclusion would have no meaningful impact on its business but would harm MSCI's reputation as a neutral index provider.

MSCI's review documents did not give company-specific explanations for the removals.

Strategy's common MSTR shares fell 2.6% in after-hours trading to $119.06. Metaplanet shares closed Wednesday down 2.53% at 231 yen in Tokyo, before MSCI announced the removals.

#5077. Nvidia preparing to oust CEO Jensen Huang after affairs: WSJ

The report about Huang’s possible departure came after the New York Times reported that he had a second extramarital affair.

Nvidia’s board is preparing to oust founder and CEO Jensen Huang after he had two extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected Chinese spy, The Wall Street Journal reported.

Directors could meet as soon as Wednesday to determine Huang’s future, the report said, citing people familiar with the matter.

His potential removal would mark a major leadership change for the chipmaker, which has become central to the artificial intelligence boom under Huang.

Cointelegraph reached out to Nvidia for comment but did not receive an immediate response.

One affair involved Alice Huang, an executive at Singapore-based data company Megaspeed, a major Nvidia customer. US intelligence officials believe she was a spy for Beijing.

The New York Times reported Wednesday that Huang also had an extramarital affair with Nvidia’s vice president of health care, Kimberly Powell, according to five people who worked at the company.

On Sunday, Huang acknowledged being unfaithful to his wife in a letter to employees, apologizing for breaching their trust.

“I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did,” he wrote.

Huang also confirmed that Nvidia’s board had launched an independent investigation into national security concerns raised in news reports. He pledged to cooperate fully and said he remained committed to the company.

Nvidia shares fell 8% to $159.76 in after-hours trading following the reports, reversing a 2.9% gain during the regular session, according to Google Finance data.

#5076. Russia’s Central Bank Clears Exchange to Open Bitcoin and Crypto Trading on Nov. 22

The Bank of Russia said Wednesday that exchanges may start organized trading in cryptocurrencies on Nov. 22, once the central bank has approved their trading rules, a step that lands just ahead of Moscow Exchange’s planned Dec. 1 bitcoin launch.

The ordinance, which amends the Bank of Russia’s rules for organized trading, was registered by the Justice Ministry on Nov. 3 and published Wednesday on the regulator’s site. It takes effect 10 days after publication, on Nov. 22.

Exchanges will have to register the brokers, trust managers and central counterparties that take part in crypto trades, keep registers of orders and deals, and monitor the activity. They must also calculate a current price, a weighted-average price, a market price and a closing price, and report trading activity to the central bank in the same way they already do for other assets.

The regulator was clear about the order of operations. Exchanges may open crypto markets from Nov. 22, it said, “but first they must agree with the regulator on the rules for conducting such trading.” It did not say whether any venue has already cleared that review.

A separate ordinance, published Nov. 6 and due to take effect Nov. 17, sets the menu for ordinary buyers. The Bank of Russia approved bitcoin, ether and Tether’s USDT for purchase by non-qualified investors, and said exchanges may admit those assets to public trading from that date.

Non-qualified investors can buy through a broker, a trust manager or a registered crypto exchange office, on an exchange or over the counter. Purchases through a single intermediary may not exceed 300,000 rubles a year, roughly $3,600 at the official exchange rate. Qualified investors can access all cryptoassets with no purchase cap.

Both groups still have to pass a knowledge test and read a risk disclosure before they can trade. The list is limited to the most liquid assets that meet the law’s tests for market capitalization, average daily volume and at least five years of pricing history on foreign exchanges.

Moscow Exchange, Russia’s largest trading venue, announced on Nov. 2 that it plans to offer bitcoin, ether and USDT trades from Dec. 1, initially as a pilot while participants connect. Trading would run on its existing stock-market systems from 6:50 a.m. to 11:50 p.m. Moscow time, with the National Clearing Centre clearing the deals and clients able to settle the same day or the next.

Bitcoin Magazine reported the Dec. 1 plan last week, including the U.S. sanctions that already cover the exchange and its clearing house. Boris Blokhin, a senior managing director at the exchange, said in early October that Moscow Exchange would not need additional licenses to start and would use a partner settlement digital depository. Wednesday’s notice still requires the central bank to sign off on the trading rules themselves. The exchange has not said publicly whether that approval is already in hand.

SPB Exchange, the St. Petersburg venue, has said it wants to start organized crypto trading by the end of the year and is building a settlement digital depository of its own, Russian newspaper Vedomosti reported in October.

The market sits on Federal Law No. 282-FZ, “On Digital Currencies and Digital Rights,” whose main provisions took effect on Sept. 1. The law licenses exchanges, brokers, managers, depositories and exchangers, recognizes crypto as property and opens a supervised channel for cross-border trade. It still bans using crypto to pay for goods and services inside Russia. Officials have spent the fall standing up the rest of the plumbing, including a self-regulatory body for crypto exchanges and digital depositories.

The test now is not whether Russia will have a legal exchange market for bitcoin, but when the first venue actually opens, and whether a 300,000-ruble retail cap is enough to pull traders off the offshore desks they already use.

#5074. A High-Severity Bug Could Have Stalled Bitcoin Mining—Bitcoin Core Has a Fix

A High-Severity Bug Could Have Stalled Bitcoin Mining—Bitcoin Core Has a Fix

Bitcoin Core developers have disclosed three security vulnerabilities in the software that runs most of the Bitcoin network, including one high-severity bug that could disrupt mining, all of which have since been fixed.

Bitcoin Core, the reference software operating the majority of the network’s nodes, classifies vulnerabilities into four tiers, from “low” to “critical,” and these fell into the middle of that range.

The most serious, tracked as CVE-2025-62861, concerns block template creation, the process miners rely on to assemble new blocks.

According to the advisory, a chain reorganization could trigger an erroneous eviction of certain transactions, corrupting a node’s mempool, its waiting room for unconfirmed transactions, so that an affected node could no longer build block templates until it was restarted. For a miner, that means a halt to the very work that earns block rewards.

A second flaw, CVE-2025-62859, could let a peer stall a node’s block processing by announcing a compact block with a manipulated witness, a form of transaction malleation.

None of the three rises to the “critical” level Bitcoin Core reserves for bugs that threaten coin theft at the protocol level, unauthorized coin creation or permanent network-wide chain splits. The developers did not report any evidence the flaws were exploited.

The disclosures come amid a broader reckoning over how quickly Bitcoin’s defenders can keep pace with a shifting threat landscape.

Bitcoin Core recently rewrote its security policy to account for AI, adding a category for “easily discoverable” vulnerabilities that can be surfaced through a simple AI prompt, which it now patches and discloses immediately rather than holding under a long embargo.

That shift reflects mounting concern that large language models have lowered the bar for finding flaws, a worry underscored by the volunteer “Bitcoin Red Team,” which has been racing to uncover AI-discoverable bugs across the ecosystem before attackers can exploit them.

The fixes themselves shipped earlier, with the advisories following under Bitcoin Core’s disclosure timeline. Node operators are generally urged to run the latest software to stay protected.

#5071. MSCI Rules Today on Cutting Strategy (MSTR) From Its Indexes After a Draft List Marks It Deleted

MSCI will say Wednesday evening whether Strategy stays in its global stock indexes, after its own potential list already marked the largest corporate bitcoin holder as Deleted.

The index provider publishes its November 2026 Index Review shortly after 5 p.m. ET. MSCI said on Oct. 15 that the review would carry the final list of current constituents hit by the new screen, with any deletions taking effect at the close of Nov. 30.

MSCI’s list of potentially impacted securities, published with the Oct. 15 decision and built on Sept. 30 financial data and Oct. 9 market values, already marks five companies as Deleted: Strategy (MSTR), uranium holder Yellow Cake, Japan’s Metaplanet, Iceland’s Skel Fjárfestingafélag and micro-cap Upexi.

Strategy is the only large-cap name in the group and by float-adjusted market value accounts for more than 90% of the five. The company held 850,364 bitcoin as of Nov. 8, acquired at an average price of $75,456 a coin.

The rule does not mention bitcoin. MSCI first checks whether a company’s operating assets exceed 50% of total assets. Issuers that fail face five financial ratios, and a company flagged on four or more of them is ineligible for the MSCI Global Investable Market Indexes.

Buffers limit turnover. A company outside the indexes cannot be added if it fails the screen in either of its two most recent annual filing periods, but a current constituent is removed only if it fails in each of them. MSCI said it may also use the most recent interim filing.

That two-filing test is the question hanging over Strategy. It adopted fair-value accounting for its bitcoin at the start of 2025, so its fiscal 2024 statements carried the coins at cost less impairment, and MSCI has not said how it treats that earlier year. A Deleted mark implies MSCI’s draft reading is that Strategy already failed both periods, but it has not published the inputs behind that placement.

Strategy has argued that it reports its bitcoin treasury as an operating segment and books related gains and losses in operating expenses.

The screen replaces an approach aimed squarely at crypto. MSCI first proposed in late 2025 to exclude companies whose digital assets made up 50% or more of total assets, then dropped that plan in January and opened a broader consultation in August. The new rule also ends the interim measures MSCI placed on those companies in October 2025, including a freeze on share-count increases and a deferral of additions.

JUST IN: MSCI decides this afternoon whether to keep Strategy and Metaplanet in its global indexes.JPMorgan estimates removal could trigger up to $2.8 billion in forced selling from passive funds 👀 pic.twitter.com/dp7R47U35W

Funds that track an MSCI index have to hold what the index holds, so a deletion would force them to sell by the Nov. 30 close. JPMorgan analysts estimated last November that an MSCI exclusion could drive about $2.8 billion of outflows from Strategy, and as much as $8.8 billion more if other index providers followed. That estimate was built on MSCI’s earlier digital-asset proposal, not the October screen, and MSCI itself has published no figure.

A removal would not delist Strategy’s stock, and nothing in the rules forces the company to sell bitcoin. The pressure would land on the shares, and through them on the premium Strategy uses to fund new purchases by selling stock. In an August letter to MSCI, the company said a deletion “would have no meaningful impact on Strategy’s business.”

Strategy’s own cushion is already thin. It bought 1,036 bitcoin for $81.9 million in the week to Nov. 8, a purchase that left its holdings below that average cost. Bitcoin traded near $73,900 Wednesday.

If Strategy fails only its latest filing rather than both, it would not be deleted this round and would instead go on a watchlist. The potential list already places 17 other constituents there, including SharpLink, TON Strategy and Solana Company.

Tokyo-listed Metaplanet faces the same Deleted mark, after it sold bitcoin last quarter to show investors it could tap its holdings for liquidity. The test now is whether Wednesday’s official list sticks with those Deleted marks, or whether MSCI pulls any of the five back onto the watchlist.

#5069. ‘Milestone quarter’: Solana Company reports Q3 revenue growth driven by staking rewards

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The digital asset treasury firm posted $3.4 million in quarterly revenue, compared with $697,000 in the same period last year.

Solana Company (HSDT) reported strong growth in its third-quarter revenue, primarily driven by staking revenue on its SOL Solana (SOL) holdings.

According to its Wednesday press release, the digital asset treasury firm posted $3.4 million in quarterly revenue, compared with $697,000 in the same period last year. Around $3.3 million of the revenue came from staking. Its year-to-date revenue reached $9.5 million.

As of Sept. 30, the company held roughly 2.4 million SOL, with a fair value of approximately $288.2 million. The firm generated an average net staking yield of 5.9% APY on its treasury holdings, outperforming the Solana network's average 4.9% APY. During the quarter, the firm earned 30,800 SOL in staking rewards, which were restaked.

Gross profit for the quarter was $2.7 million, or an approximate 82% gross margin, compared with $594,000 in the third quarter of the prior year.

"This was a milestone quarter for Solana Company," said CEO Joseph Chee. "Our Pacific Backbone infrastructure went live, institutions entrusted us with a million SOL of third-party stake, and that confidence is already flowing through to our revenue."

Pacific Backbone is Solana Company's staking and validator infrastructure cluster in the Asia-Pacific region, connecting key financial hubs including Seoul, Tokyo, Singapore, and Hong Kong to support Solana staking and validation.

Pacific Backbone operations had about 1.3 million SOL of total stake delegated as of the end of Q3, including 1 million SOL from third parties. Total assets stood at $292.6 million.

"We are building the bridge that brings Asia's financial institutions onto Solana, and with our new headquarters in Hong Kong, we will be building it right where the demand is," Chee added.

The company also priced an approximately $15 million registered direct offering at a 5% premium to net asset value per share and used the proceeds to purchase about 125,000 SOL. It repurchased roughly $2.8 million of Class A common stock during the quarter.

Dollar stablecoins, Sam Lyman says, are “like Starlink for the US dollar system”: if you have an internet connection, you can access the dollar. #5066. Suiet wallet to shut down in latest exit from Sui ecosystem

The self-custodial wallet will keep its API services running through Nov. 14. Sui’s DeFi total value locked has fallen more than 22% over the past month.

Cryptocurrency wallet Suiet is shutting down, adding to a series of closures and service withdrawals in the Sui ecosystem.

Suiet, a self-custodial wallet built for the Sui blockchain, said in a notice that it will stop updating and supporting its wallet, with API services set to end after Nov. 14. The shutdown comes as the Sui network has seen a sharp decline in decentralized finance (DeFi) activity.

Sui’s total value locked (TVL) stood at about $413 million on Wednesday, down more than 22% over the past month, according to DefiLlama data. Data from Token Terminal shows that Sui averaged about 128,000 daily active users in October, down from 601,000 in November 2025 – or around a 79% drop.

Meanwhile, other recent closures have also raised concerns about the costs and challenges of keeping projects running on the Sui network.

“Sui’s problems seem to mostly be of an economic nature resulting from expensive engineering costs and low user volume rather than protocol failures,” Kadan Stadelmann, former chief technology officer of Komodo Platform, told Cointelegraph. “No flaw in the system’s core design has been found.”

Cointelegraph reached out to the Sui Foundation and Suiet for comment but had not received responses by publication.

Related: Sui DeFi protocol Full Sail to wind down after Switchboard incident

Suiet’s shutdown immediately follows the Sui-based hardware wallet project Suiball also announcing its closure on Nov. 10. Meanwhile wallet provider Phantom ended support for Sui in September.

DeFi project Full Sail also shut down following a security incident involving Switchboard that caused about $91,000 in losses.

Despite the decline in active users, transactions on Sui have increased, reaching 324 million in October, up from 101 million in November 2025, according to Token Terminal. However, more transactions do not necessarily mean more users, and wallet providers must still decide whether supporting the network is worth the cost, according to Stadelmann.

“Multi-chain wallets have expansive attack surfaces, and Sui’s approach adds costs for vendors who can take their business to less capital intensive chains,” he said. “Any individual failure of a multi-chain wallet is more a reflection of the risks rather than an indictment of any given protocol.”

Sui has also experienced four network outages in 2026, resulting in around 22 hours of combined downtime, according to reports published by the Sui Foundation.

The network stopped processing transactions for about six hours in January, followed by three more outages on May 28 and 29. The May disruptions were linked to software bugs, including problems introduced by a network update, the Sui Foundation said at the time.

By comparison, rival blockchain Solana has not recorded a network-wide outage since February 2024, when it went offline for nearly five hours. Meanwhile, Aptos, which uses the same underlying smart contract programming language as Sui, last reported a mainnet outage in October 2023.

Network outages are significant because they can prevent DeFi users from trading, transferring funds or managing positions, although the Sui Foundation said no user funds were at risk during its May disruptions.

#5065. What Are Solana Privacy Rings? Inside Helius’ Privacy Protocol for Solana

Every transaction on Solana is public by default. Anyone can look up a wallet address and see its balance, what it has sent and received, and when. That transparency is useful for verifying the network, but a problem for anyone who doesn't want their finances on display: a business paying salaries, a trader building a position, or an individual who simply doesn't want strangers seeing their savings.

Rings are programmable, onchain environments for encrypting money, markets, and finance - without ever leaving Solana

Now live on mainnet. pic.twitter.com/ezCIUCjcbI

Rings support SOL and other tokens on Solana, including SPL and Token-2022 tokens. Users keep custody of their funds throughout, since every transaction needs the owner's signature.

Crucially, the private balances live on the same network as everything else. Private transactions execute directly on Solana and encrypted data is stored on Solana's ledger, with no separate layer-2 network or sequencer in between. That means private balances sit alongside the same stablecoins, tokenized assets and DeFi liquidity as public ones.

Each private transfer comes with a zero-knowledge proof, a piece of cryptography that lets the network confirm the transfer is valid, for example that the sender actually had the funds, without revealing the numbers involved. Rings use a type of zero-knowledge proof called a Groth16 zk-SNARK, which Helius says can be generated in tens of milliseconds, either by a service provider or on the user's own device. Each private transfer settles in a single Solana transaction.

Users read their own encrypted balances with a viewing key. They can decrypt their balance locally, or let a service provider decrypt it and serve it to them, and they can share a key for a single transaction with an auditor or counterparty without exposing the rest of their history.

Moving funds into a Ring from an ordinary public wallet, or back out again, is visible onchain, including the asset and amount. But users who on-ramp from fiat currency straight into a private balance, through a supported provider, keep the asset and amount hidden from the start, according to Helius.

Because Custom Rings interoperate with the default Ring, regulated firms can keep their activity confidential from the market while still sharing the same liquidity as everyone else.

Private transfers are just the start. Solana programs can hold encrypted balances in private escrows and release them when set conditions are met, opening the way for:

At launch, Decrypt, mining and staking app ORE and cross-chain service Aurora Intents were using the default Ring, while Backpack, DFlow, Jupiter, Squads and Titan were among the teams working on integrations.

Helius says the core programs and zero-knowledge circuits have been audited by security firms Certora, OtterSec and ZK Security. Engineers from the Solana Foundation, Blueshift, Zcash and other teams took part in the "trusted setup," the one-off ceremony that generates the parameters the proofs rely on.

The code is open source, published on GitHub under the Apache 2.0 license, so anyone can inspect how it works.

Formal verification, a mathematical check that the circuits behave exactly as intended, is still underway and expected to finish by the end of 2026. Solana's own documentation currently describes Rings as being in beta.

#5060. Bitcoin Faces First Back-to-Back Annual Losses in History, But There's a Catch

Bitcoin Faces First Back-to-Back Annual Losses in History, But There's a Catch

Bitcoin is flirting with a historical first that bulls probably don’t want to hear: Two consecutive years of losses, something the cryptocurrency has never experienced in its 18-year history.

The world's largest cryptocurrency finished 2025 down 6.34% and has lost another 15.65% so far in 2026, trading around $73,850 on November 10. If Bitcoin fails to recover above its annual opening price of roughly $87,500 before December 31, it will close two calendar years in the red for the first time.

Historical annual returns show Bitcoin has experienced several brutal crashes since its creation, but each negative calendar year has been followed by a positive one. Even the disastrous 2018 and 2022 bear markets failed to produce consecutive annual losses.

The historical pattern is clear. Its significance, however, deserves a closer look.

Bitcoin's previous annual crashes were considerably more painful than its current losing streak. The cryptocurrency plunged 73.56% in 2018 and another 64.27% in 2022, wiping out most of investors' holdings in each respective year.

By comparison, Bitcoin's 6.34% decline in 2025 was relatively mild. Add the roughly 16% loss recorded this year, and the combined decline comes to approximately 21% after accounting for compounding.

That's a fraction of the losses investors endured in previous crypto winters, despite the market potentially recording a longer sequence of negative annual returns.

Bitcoin's performance in 2024 also provides some perspective. The cryptocurrency surged more than 120% that year, meaning investors who bought at the beginning of 2024 would still be sitting on substantial gains despite the subsequent decline.

The picture changes considerably when measuring losses from the market's actual peak instead of calendar-year boundaries.

Bitcoin reached approximately $126,000 in October 2025 before crashing below $58,000 during 2026. That represented a decline exceeding 50%, a significantly more painful move than the 21% combined loss reflected in the two annual candlesticks.

Analysts have pointed to Bitcoin's increasingly institutional investor base, spot exchange-traded funds, and deeper market liquidity as potential explanations for its comparatively smaller bear-market drawdowns.

Those structural changes may help explain why Bitcoin's current market cycle looks different from previous ones, although they have not eliminated major price crashes.

There is also a distinction between the length of a losing streak and the severity of the losses. A market can decline slowly over several calendar years without inflicting the same damage as a single catastrophic year.

And Bitcoin has already demonstrated that a bad year doesn't necessarily translate into a prolonged downturn. The cryptocurrency gained roughly 92% in 2019 following its 2018 collapse and surged more than 155% in 2023 after its disastrous 2022 performance.

For now, Bitcoin has 50 days left in 2026 to avoid its first back-to-back annual losses. A third consecutive decline in 2027 would extend the streak further, but that scenario is still hypothetical.

At its November 10 price of approximately $73,850, Bitcoin would need to gain about 18.5% to recover its 2026 opening price and finish the year in positive territory.

#5059. MSCI confirms Strategy’s removal from indexes at end of November

Strategy, Metaplanet and Upexi are marked for deletion in MSCI’s final list, with changes taking effect after the Nov. 30 close.

Index provider MSCI has confirmed Bitcoin treasury company Strategy will be removed from its global equity indexes, following months of debate over whether crypto treasury firms qualify as operating businesses.

MSCI’s final list of affected securities, released as part of its November index review, lists “STRATEGY A” with the action “Deleted.” Japan’s Metaplanet and crypto treasury company Upexi are also marked for deletion, while Ether treasury firm Sharplink is on a watchlist.

Strategy shares fell 1.65% in after-hours trading on Wednesday.

The changes take effect after market close on Nov. 30, under the implementation timetable MSCI announced in October.

Removal could prompt funds tracking the affected benchmarks to sell the companies’ shares, reducing a source of passive investment demand.

As Cointelegraph reported in October, JPMorgan previously estimated that Strategy’s exclusion could trigger roughly $2.8 billion in selling.

Strategy opposed the proposal, arguing that it repackaged MSCI’s earlier effort to exclude digital asset treasury companies and injected policy judgments into index construction.

The company nevertheless played down the potential disruption. In its consultation response, Strategy said MSCI-linked funds held approximately 13 million shares, representing 3.1% of its basic shares outstanding and less than one day’s trading volume.

#5058. Ethlabs proposes advancing Ethereum’s post-quantum to 2027, citing AI acceleration

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The group said it aims to make Ethereum post-AI by 2027 as well, resistant to cryptographic risks that AI development is bringing.

Ethlabs proposed bringing forward Ethereum's Ethereum (ETH) post-quantum upgrade to 2027, arguing that accelerating quantum timelines makes earlier action necessary.

In a post on the Ethereum Magicians forum, Ethlabs said the upgrade — which it calls "pqai27" — is needed because AI is accelerating the timelines for quantum and cryptographic risks, while also creating an opportunity for Ethereum to lock in its position as a credible decentralized root of trust.

Ethlabs noted that the U.S. government moved its quantum risk goal from 2035 to 2030 and that Google now aims to be post-quantum by 2029. The Ethereum Foundation previously established December 2029 for Ethereum's full post-quantum readiness.

"Being post-quantum is not enough; we intend on being post-AI too," Ethlabs wrote. It defined post-AI as cryptography that rests on as few assumptions as possible, and clients hardened against frontier models that find bugs at scale, using formal verification and Ethereum's client diversity.

It also pointed to AI solving hundreds of open mathematics problems and finding roughly 1,000 vulnerabilities in Linux, noting that "the security of our cryptography is itself an open maths problem."

"You don't have to believe in any single one of these threats," Ethlabs said. "What has changed is that it has become much harder to reason when any of them will arrive. Even at today's levels, AI is accelerating all of them."

It distinguished two levels of readiness for 2027: "pqai-ready," where consensus is post-quantum, and users have a post-quantum route for their accounts while the network continues at scaled capacity, and "pqai-scaled," in which the network operates at full capacity with post-quantum transactions and data availability.

Moving the target timeline to 2027 will "future-proof" Ethereum's position as "the credible decentralized root of trust" for the global economy, the proposal said, adding that its resilience against AI-led uncertainty will be Ethereum's next killer feature.

"With attacks on software taking off, security and uptime will be more in demand than ever," the proposal said. "Now is the cheapest time to commit. A pivot later, when we are deep into shipping the currently planned fork, would be far more costly than today."

Ethlabs said Ethereum already has a clear technical path, built around hash-function-based cryptography with minimal assumptions, years of prior research, a dedicated post-quantum team, and embedded formal verification practices.

To meet the 2027 target, Ethlabs said upcoming hard forks, including Hegotá, should be reassessed so that anything not on the critical path to post-quantum and post-AI readiness is reconsidered. The group stated it will withdraw quick slots, a proposal to make the network's block production faster, from Hegotá and dedicate its resources to pqai27.

"We don't take this decision lightly," it wrote. "We pushed for quick slots because our survey showed that the community clearly wants them, and we are ready to defend this decision to the people who trusted us to deliver them."

Founded by former Ethereum Foundation researchers, Ethlabs launched earlier this year as a nonprofit organization for Ethereum research and development.

#5054. Deutsche Bank Goes Live With Crypto Custody After a Five-Year Buildup

Deutsche Bank Goes Live With Crypto Custody After a Five-Year Buildup

Five years after it first floated the idea, Germany’s largest lender is now holding Bitcoin for clients.

Deutsche Bank said Wednesday that its Digital Asset Custody service has gone live for paying clients, giving institutional and corporate customers in Europe a regulated way to store and transfer Bitcoin, Ethereum and three stablecoins: Circle’s USDC and EURC, plus EURAU.

The bank manages wallets and private keys on clients’ behalf. For now, the service covers custody and transfers, with tokenized financial instruments on its roadmap.

The service is aimed at clients of Deutsche Bank’s corporate and investment banking units, including corporations, asset managers, hedge funds, custodians, brokers and sovereign institutions, according to the bank’s September announcement of the plan.

Its setup includes hardware-based key protection, multi-person approvals and separate warm and cold storage, the bank said.

“In the medium term we aim to deliver a fully integrated, regulated ecosystem that supports the custody, tokenisation, and settlement of digital assets,” said Paul Maley, Deutsche Bank’s global head of trust and securities services.

Deutsche Bank also signed a memorandum of understanding with Deutsche Asset Management, the majority-owned money manager formerly known as DWS, to explore working together on tokenized money market funds, crypto exchange-traded commodities and stablecoins.

The asset manager oversaw about 1.22 trillion euros as of Sept. 30. The bank has also said it plans to expand the custody service to Asia.

The launch caps a long road, going back to at least 2021 when Deutsche Bank began readying a crypto custody and trading platform. Then, in 2023, the bank applied for a digital asset custody license in Germany. Last year, reports said the bank planned to build the service with technology from crypto exchange Bitpanda, alongside its longtime partner Taurus.

Deutsche Bank received authorization from German regulator BaFin under the EU’s Markets in Crypto-Assets regulation, or MiCA, which requires crypto service providers to be licensed. The bank cautioned that its custody service doesn’t protect clients against swings in the value of digital assets, fraud or failures by other market participants.

The custody launch isn’t Deutsche Bank’s only crypto bet. The bank is among a group of global lenders, including Goldman Sachs and Citi, backing a $270 million challenge to crypto’s dominant stablecoin issuers.

#5053. Chainstack says AI-powered security breach exposed customer data; threat now contained

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The blockchain infrastructure provider said it is treating node credentials as exposed and asking customers to rotate them and change passwords.

Blockchain infrastructure provider Chainstack revealed it experienced an AI-powered security breach in late October that exposed customer names, emails, and password hashes.

In an updated blog post on Tuesday, Chainstack said the incident has been contained and that customer-facing services continued to operate normally throughout the breach.

The company noted that an attacker first gained access to a development environment on Oct. 26, and reached parts of the production environment on Oct. 29. The firm then detected the intrusion and cut the access paths on Oct. 31. It later finalized the investigation on Nov. 2.

According to the post, the attacker accessed the system via an internet-exposed Grafana instance in a development cluster that was deployed for testing with its default administrator credentials and without an additional layer of protection.

The attacker then obtained machine credentials that reached production, and used them to access the database network. Chainstack said the attacker used the ARTEX AI agent framework to carry out the breach.

The Block has reached out to Chainstack for further comment.

Following the incident, Chainstack said it is currently treating node credentials as exposed and notifying customers to rotate them and change passwords. The company added that customer nodes, console, and API were not modified. Self-service rotation for node credentials is currently available in the console without downtime, per the post.

Chainstack also warned customers to watch for phishing attempts. "Chainstack will never ask for passwords, tokens, or keys," the team said.

The incident comes amid a wave of cyberattacks and security breaches as AI tools become increasingly capable. Earlier this week, crosschain interoperability protocol Across suffered a domain takeover, and later restored control of its website and resumed normal operations.

#5049. Tether's Tiny AI Agent Beats Models 2.5 Times Its Size at Controlling Your Phone

Tether's Tiny AI Agent Beats Models 2.5 Times Its Size at Controlling Your Phone

Tether, the company behind the world's largest stablecoin, USDT, is getting surprisingly good at making tiny AI models punch above their weight.

The company on Wednesday unveiled QVAC BotPsy, an 800-million-parameter AI agent designed to carry out everyday tasks on personal devices. Parameters are essentially the knobs that determine how much an AI model can do, and more of them generally translate to improved performance. And for reference, frontier AI models today run in the trillions of parameters.

In the company's own testing, Tether’s relatively tiny model outperformed competitors with up to 2.5 times as many parameters, suggesting that AI assistants may not need enormous models to get things done.

Unlike traditional chatbots that simply respond to questions, BotPsy is agentic, meaning it’s designed to take action. It can identify which tools an application needs, execute commands in the right order, and adjust when something goes wrong.

For example, Tether described a benchmark scenario in which an AI assistant needed to send a message but encountered two problems: Wi-Fi was disabled and the inbox was full. BotPsy turned on Wi-Fi, cleared enough storage, and completed the task, while the underlying model repeatedly attempted the same failed operation.

That's a fairly mundane achievement, but it's precisely the kind of problem that makes AI agents useful outside a chatbot window.

BotPsy is built on Alibaba's Qwen3.5-0.8B architecture, a relatively small language model that Tether trained further to specialize in interacting with software tools. Its 800 million parameters represent the numerical values the model learned during training, and a smaller parameter count generally means less memory is needed to run it.

According to Tether's technical documentation, BotPsy scored 44.0% on BFCL v4, a benchmark that evaluates an AI system's ability to select and execute software functions across different tasks and conversations. Scores reflect a weighted percentage of successful performance across tool-use categories.

The underlying Qwen3.5-0.8B model scored 23.4%, while the considerably larger Qwen3.5-2B reached 37.7%.

BotPsy also scored 58.7% on ACEBench, which evaluates the accuracy of function calls, including selecting the appropriate tools and arguments. Its base model managed 30.1%, while the 2-billion-parameter competitor scored 55.4%.

The company tested five models across six tool-use evaluations, with BotPsy posting the highest results on five. Its average score across those tests reached 45.0%, compared with 38.8% for the larger Qwen model.

BotPsy is the latest addition to QVAC, Tether's effort to build an AI ecosystem that runs on devices controlled by users rather than relying entirely on centralized cloud infrastructure.

The company first introduced QVAC in May 2025, positioning the project as an alternative to AI services operated by technology giants. Its longer-term ambitions included autonomous agents capable of interacting with applications and potentially making payments using Bitcoin and USDT.

Tether expanded that initiative in October 2025 with a 41-billion-token dataset for training science and engineering models, alongside QVAC Workbench, an application that lets users run AI models locally.

Earlier this year, Tether released its SDK, providing developers with tools to build offline AI applications across mobile and desktop operating systems.

The company also developed QVAC MedPsy, a medical AI system designed to run on consumer hardware. Its 1.7-billion-parameter version outperformed Google's much larger MedGemma-27B on HealthBench Hard, a test of realistic clinical conversations evaluated using physician-developed criteria.

BotPsy is currently available as an open-weight model under the Apache 2.0 license, meaning developers can download, modify, and integrate it into their own applications. Tether has also released compressed versions intended to reduce memory requirements for local deployment.

The company has not announced a release date for a finished consumer assistant powered by BotPsy. Its published model card also says the training code and training data are not included in the release.

#5048. MSCI Removes Strategy (MSTR) From Its USA Index, Forcing Index Funds to Sell by Nov. 30

MSCI will remove Strategy, the largest corporate bitcoin holder, from the MSCI USA Index as of the close of Nov. 30, the index provider said Wednesday, after a new screen classified the company as a non-operating issuer.

The decision is part of MSCI’s November 2026 Index Review, posted shortly after 5 p.m. ET. Official additions and deletions lists show Strategy (MSTR) out of its USA Index, which also takes it out of the broader MSCI World and ACWI indexes. The same review deletes Japan’s Metaplanet from the MSCI Japan Small Cap Index and uranium holder Yellow Cake from the MSCI United Kingdom Small Cap Index, and it removes Iceland’s Skel Fjárfestingafélag from the MSCI Iceland Frontier Markets Small Cap Index and micro-cap Upexi from the USA Micro Cap Index. All five had already been marked Deleted on MSCI’s potential-impact list, built on Sept. 30 financial data and Oct. 9 market values.

Strategy is the only large-cap name in the group and, on that October list, accounted for more than 90% of the five companies’ combined float-adjusted market value. The company held 850,364 bitcoin as of Nov. 8, acquired at an average price of $75,456 a coin, or $64.16 billion in aggregate.

Strategy shares closed at $122.35, up 1.06%, then slipped 1.51% to $120.50 in after-hours trading as of 5:34 p.m. ET.

The rule does not mention bitcoin. The index first checks whether a company’s operating assets equal at least 50% of total assets. Issuers that fail that core screen face five financial ratios, and a company flagged on four or more of them is ineligible for the MSCI Global Investable Market Indexes.

Buffers limit turnover. A company outside the indexes cannot be added if it fails the ineligibility screen in either of its two most recent annual filing periods, but a current constituent is removed only if it fails in each of them. The index said it may also use the most recent interim filing.

MSCI also said Wednesday that the same non-operating treatment will apply to the MSCI US Index Series.

The screen replaces an approach aimed squarely at crypto. The index first proposed in late 2025 to exclude companies whose digital assets made up 50% or more of total assets, then dropped that plan in January and opened a broader consultation in August. It adopted the new methodology on Oct. 15, with minor modifications, and said it would end the interim measures it had placed on those companies in October 2025, including a freeze on share-count increases and a deferral of additions.

Funds that track an MSCI index have to hold what the index holds, so they will have to sell the deleted stocks by the Nov. 30 close. JPMorgan analysts estimated last November that an exclusion could drive about $2.8 billion of outflows from Strategy, and as much as $8.8 billion more if other index providers followed. That estimate was built on MSCI’s earlier digital-asset proposal, not the October screen, and MSCI itself has published no figure.

A removal does not delist Strategy’s stock, and nothing in the rules forces the company to sell bitcoin. The pressure lands on the shares, and through them on the premium Strategy uses to fund new purchases by selling stock.

In an August letter, the company said a deletion “would have no meaningful impact on Strategy’s business.” Strategy has also said funds tracking MSCI’s global investable indexes hold 3.1% of its basic shares outstanding, and that it reports its bitcoin treasury as an operating segment under U.S. GAAP.

Seventeen other constituents were on the potential watchlist, including SharpLink, TON Strategy and Solana Company. Those names do not appear among Wednesday’s published deletions.

Tokyo-listed Metaplanet is the other bitcoin treasury firm in the deleted group, after it sold bitcoin last quarter to show investors it could tap its holdings for liquidity. Strategy bought 1,036 bitcoin for $81.9 million in the week to Nov. 8, a purchase that left its holdings below that average cost.

#5047. Binance CSO unwittingly foreshadowed $6.3B hack tactic months before attack

In a July interview, Binance chief security officer Jimmy Su explained how fake coding assessments could compromise employee devices, a tactic later used in the exchange’s record-breaking hack.

Months before Binance lost $6.3 billion to a hack, its chief security officer described to Cointelegraph how a fake job interview could give attackers a foothold inside the exchange — closely resembling one of the entry methods later used against the exchange.

During a July interview about Binance’s response to rising security threats, Jimmy Su told Cointelegraph that fake job interviews were “something we worry about a lot internally,” explaining how attackers could tailor a job opportunity to an employee before persuading them to run code as part of a technical assessment.

“And once that gets executed, the device is compromised and then they can start harvesting credentials,” Su said, adding that attackers could then use that to penetrate further into the network.

Su’s detailed warning about malicious coding assessments was not included in Cointelegraph’s July coverage, but takes on new significance following the biggest crypto exchange hack in history.

In September, a Binance software engineer downloaded a malicious coding assignment onto his work computer during a fake job interview. That compromise became part of the multistage attack that culminated in October’s theft, according to Binance’s preliminary investigation.

During the interview with Cointelegraph, Su had also anticipated why security tools might miss such a trap. A technical assessment could involve seemingly legitimate steps, such as downloading a code repository from GitHub, he explained in July.

“Each step is actually legitimate on its own. So a lot of the anti-virus tools may not be able to detect it and flag it,” he said.

Binance’s investigation subsequently described a similar detection failure, saying the assignment’s setup process executed malware that evaded its endpoint security tools through obfuscation and other evasion techniques.

Su told Cointelegraph that it was Binance’s responsibility to contain the risk when employees pursued outside opportunities on work computers.

“Of course employees can explore outside opportunities. What we do worry about is that they do so on the company device,” he said. If an employee fell for a fake interview, he added, “the onus is on us to detect it,” and to “stop the actual execution of that code.”

Related: North Korean hackers create memecoins to launder funds from Binance hack: Elliptic

Binance was already testing employees against fake recruiters. Su said its internal red team had been conducting monthly phishing simulations for three to four years, with remedial training for employees who failed.

“In the beginning, the security hygiene left a lot to be desired. But after this amount of time, the company has improved significantly,” he said.

Kevin Gibson of recruitment firm Proof of Search told Cointelegraph that the threat is also changing how some crypto employers assess prospective hires, with clients increasingly wanting to know whether candidates have been met in person.

“A number of our clients are now asking us straight up whether we’ve met a candidate in person,” Gibson said, adding that candidates personally met by a third-party recruiter have an advantage.

Employees, like in Binance’s case, can also become targets.

“In crypto, a dodgy job offer or a ‘quick catch-up call’ can be the first step in a multimillion-dollar hit,” he said.

Binance said it blocked 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threats so far this year, which is roughly one attack every five days.

Following October’s attack, Binance said it had tightened device controls, rebuilt affected wallet systems and internal AI tooling, strengthened access controls and revoked compromised credentials.

It also said it was increasing investment in endpoint protection and developer-environment security, and engaging independent cybersecurity experts to review the incident. Some technical details and security measures would remain confidential, it said.

The fund opened to investors on Nov. 2 and is available to qualified and professional investors in approved markets.

Sygnum has launched the ETH Alpha Fund SP, a market-neutral fund for professional investors targeting annual returns of 8% to 10% in ETH, net of fees.

The fund combines native ETH staking with options premiums, cross-venue arbitrage and derivatives carry and funding strategies, with all gains and losses realized in ETH rather than dollars.

"Institutions that hold ETH for the long term increasingly think in ETH, not dollars, and judge their year by whether they end it holding more," said Markus Hammerli, head of asset management and ETH Alpha Fund lead at Sygnum. "We built the fund around that measure, adding in-kind income strategies to staking within the safeguards of a regulated structure."

The fund opened to investors on Nov. 2, 2026, and is available to qualified and professional investors in approved markets, including Switzerland. Institutions can also gain ETH exposure through spot ether ETFs.

Ethereum staking reached 43.8 million ETH, or nearly 36% of supply, by the end of September, while average annualized staking yields declined to 2.63%.

Thomas Brunner, Sygnum Bank's head of custody and staking, discussed institutional demand for ETH staking and the growing importance of staking rewards to long-term ETH holders in an interview with The Block in July.

"Almost no one is un-staking, which points to genuine conviction," Brunner said at the time. "The entry queue measures as much plumbing as demand."
