#4996. 7 Best Oushak Rugs Fit for Royalty, Vetted by AD (2026)

Few home furnishings can make an ordinary room feel quite so grand as Oushak rugs. Named for the Turkish town of Uşak, where the weaving tradition began, these hand-knotted floor coverings gained popularity during the Ottoman Empire and were eventually exported across Europe. Their designs are recognizable for their large-scale medallions, geometric borders, and floral motifs, many of which drew inspiration from Ottoman art and architecture. The result is a style that’s decorative but never overly fussy, which helps to explain how it has withstood the test of time.

An Oushak rug can sit comfortably beneath a traditional dining table, add some contrast to a pared-back living room, or bring some personality to an otherwise neutral bedroom. And while antique examples are certainly worth seeking out, plenty of modern Oushak rugs capture the same old-world grandeur. Put simply, you may not be a sultan, but you can certainly decorate like one.

This one-of-a-kind, 100% wool rug is hand-knotted in Taşpinar, Turkey. Its intricate pattern, light pink palette, and low-pile height of half a centimeter give it a lived-in feel that works particularly well alongside more contemporary furniture. The wool is naturally durable, so even with a little wear, the rug has plenty of life left in it.

Bring a little more floral to your floors with this lavender Oushak. Its vintage-inspired pattern is full of the botanical motifs that make Turkish rugs so distinctive, while the soft color protects its relaxed aura from feeling overly ornate. Hand-knotted from New Zealand wool, the floor covering adds just enough pattern to a room without taking over. Basically, it’s the kind of rug that makes you want to kick off your shoes and stay awhile.

This cotton-candy-hued Oushak rug, another flower-filled masterpiece, features scattered palmettes and geometric shapes that draw on the rich history of Turkish patternmaking. Handmade by artisans in India from a blend of New Zealand wool, cotton, and polyester, and available in a variety of sizes to fit many spaces, the rug’s plush feel and storied patina make it a beautiful foundation for any room.

This Oushak-inspired rug pairs symmetrical designs with a sky-blue palette, bringing brightness to any room. Think of it as artwork you can touch or, rather, walk all over. Simply place it down and let the compliments roll in. It’s meticulously handwoven from high-quality wool, making it a durable choice for high-traffic areas, and because it comes in several sizes, its an especially versatile choice.

If you’re searching for a floor covering that has the essence of an antique Persian rug, but you aren’t quite ready to worry about spills, look no further. While this option features a delicate floral pattern, its tufted polyester construction makes it a more resilient selection for the busier corners of your home. It’s machine washable up to the 8-foot-by-10-foot size (larger versions are still washable but may require a commercial machine), and is available in area rug, round, and runner formats. It’s a particularly good pick for busy households where a beautiful rug has to do more than just look pretty.

For those who prefer more subtle patterns, this simple but sophisticated hand-knotted Oushak rug is a lovely option. Its understated mix of cream, brown, green, and blue complements a soft, slightly faded finish that gives it the appeal of a vintage find.

This Oushak rug looks like it belongs to another millennium and should live in the Metropolitan Museum of Art with its perfectly faded medallion pattern and ornamental border. But it’s actually brand new—and an affordable option for just about any home. Its muted palette can skew bohemian or coastal, or simply bring a little intrigue to a cozy space of any style.

#4993. DTCC to Bring Tokenized Stocks and Treasuries to Solana in 2027

DTCC to Bring Tokenized Stocks and Treasuries to Solana in 2027

The Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation (DTCC), the backbone of U.S. securities settlement, plans to bring its tokenized securities to Solana, it announced on Thursday.

The firm will connect the DTCC Tokenization Service (DTS) to the Solana network, allowing eligible assets held by its Depository Trust Company (DTC) subsidiary to be converted into tokens on Solana. The integration is planned for the second half of 2027.

BREAKING: @The_DTCC to tokenize custodied assets on Solana. announced plans to connect the DTCC Tokenization Service to Solana, enabling the tokenization of eligible DTC-custodied assets on the network.

The integration is planned for the second half of 2027, further advancing… pic.twitter.com/gXszWFgUOX

"DTCC is bringing regulated, DTC-tokenized securities to another major blockchain ecosystem, giving market participants greater choice in where they access and utilize tokenized assets," DTCC CEO Frank La Salla said.

DTS went live on October 28 on the Canton Network and DTCC's own private blockchain, built on the Besu framework, with more networks due to be added in 2027, according to DTCC's launch announcement. The service creates tokenized "digital twins" of securities held at DTC, which custodies more than $114 trillion in assets. Solana joins Stellar and Circle's Arc blockchain, which are expected to be added in 2027, according to a DTCC fact sheet.

Under a no-action letter from the SEC, the service is limited to a defined set of highly liquid assets, including Russell 1000 stocks, ETFs tracking major indexes, and U.S. Treasuries. Morgan Stanley said last week it had tokenized U.S. stocks and Treasuries through the service.

Tokens issued on Solana "will retain the same investor protections, ownership rights and regulatory safeguards that apply to traditionally held securities," said Brian Steele, president of DTCC Clearing & Securities Services.

Solana has been actively courting traditional finance in recent months, with Solana Foundation head of institutional Nick Ducoff arguing that, "Institutions choose Solana because it already runs real financial activity at scale." He added that institutions are "already issuing, settling and deploying capital" on the network, in a tweet shared by Solana's official account.

Last month, the Solana Foundation unveiled an open standard for institutional settlement developed with input from J.P. Morgan, and the network completed its Alpenglow upgrade, cutting the time it takes for transactions to become final to roughly 150 milliseconds.

Solana is also lining up more venues for tokenized stocks. Trading platform Formation has notified the SEC of plans to trade tokenized U.S. stocks on Solana, starting with Galaxy and Forward Industries.

#4990. Uber’s UK banking partner Griffin raises $20M for stablecoin services

The Series B valued Griffin at more than $100 million and will help fund stablecoin reserve services and onchain cross-border payments.

UK-based digital bank Griffin raised $20 million in a Series B round led by Octagon Partners, valuing the company at over $100 million.

The funding will support Griffin’s plans to expand into card issuance and stablecoin services, according to a Thursday press release.

Griffin plans to let companies offer branded retail and corporate debit cards through its platform, with credit cards to follow. The bank also plans to expand its stablecoin reserve services and add onchain cross-border payments.

Griffin’s said its banking platform serves more than 300,000 retail and business customers through more than 80 companies. They include Uber, which uses Griffin to provide bank accounts to UK drivers.

Griffin received its UK banking license in March 2024. The bank said recurring revenue at its September fiscal year-end was 3.2 times the prior year’s level, based on unaudited accounts.

Paraag Davé, managing partner at Octagon, said he expects Griffin’s annual recurring revenue to exceed 10 million British pounds soon.

#4988. SoFi Brings Its Bank-Issued Stablecoin to Polygon’s Payments Stack

SoFi Brings Its Bank-Issued Stablecoin to Polygon’s Payments Stack

SoFi is widening the reach of its digital dollar, bringing SoFiUSD, the first stablecoin issued by a U.S. national bank, onto Polygon’s payments infrastructure as banks and fintechs push stablecoins deeper into everyday money movement.

Polygon Labs said Thursday that SoFiUSD is now live on Polygon Open Money Stack, a payments layer that bundles fiat on- and off-ramps, embedded wallets and cross-chain settlement into a single integration.

The move adds Polygon alongside Ethereum and Solana, the networks SoFiUSD already runs on, and lets SoFi’s more than 17 million members buy, sell, hold, and convert the token on Polygon directly in the SoFi app, without opening a separate crypto wallet or manually bridging assets between chains.

“SoFi is well positioned to bring stablecoins into mainstream finance because it combines the regulatory foundation of a national bank with the reach of a major consumer and enterprise technology platform,” said Marc Boiron, CEO of Polygon Labs.

The expansion lands as stablecoins move beyond crypto trading into the plumbing used to settle card payments, pay businesses, and send money across borders.

On Polygon Open Money Stack, SoFiUSD joins USDC, USDT and PayPal’s PYUSD, and can connect to the wallets, banking rails, and local currencies businesses need for payouts, settlement and cross-border transfers, opening up uses well beyond consumer trading.

The token is redeemable one-to-one for U.S. dollars and issued by SoFi Bank, which is regulated by the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency.

SoFi launched SoFiUSD late last year across Ethereum and Solana as the first stablecoin from a U.S. national bank, and it already settles the company’s entire card program over Mastercard’s network. The firm has been building out a broader digital-money suite, recently launching SoFi Earn, a tokenized deposit that pays 4.40% yield and converts one-to-one into SoFiUSD.

For Polygon, the addition deepens a payments business that has become one of the network’s calling cards.

Polygon recently extended Open Money Stack to Solana, and the chain has processed more than $3 trillion in lifetime stablecoin transfer volume, according to the company. Visa and Mastercard support stablecoin settlement on Polygon, while Stripe uses it to route USDC payments for merchants in more than 150 countries.

SoFiUSD is a payment stablecoin, not a bank deposit, and is not FDIC- or SIPC-insured, bank-guaranteed or legal tender.

#4986. The 9 Most Walkable Cities in the US

Anyone who’s been to the pump lately could appreciate a little less reliance on cars. And while life in the most walkable cities in the US can mean saving on gas (not to mention car insurance, parking, and potential parking tickets), getting around on foot isn’t only about your budget. Walkability “can benefit everything from household finances and health to the environment and overall quality of life,” says Daryl Fairweather, chief economist at Redfin, which has been following and analyzing walkability for years with their Walk Score tool. “There’s also a housing-market component: People tend to value the convenience and access that comes with living in a walkable neighborhood, and that desirability can translate into high home values. Walkable neighborhoods also create more opportunities for people to interact with local businesses, use public spaces and resources, and connect with their neighbors,” says Fairweather.

To determine the country’s most walkable cities, we’ve reviewed recent studies, like the one from Only in Your State, a travel and discovery platform, plus talked to realtors, residents, and tourism experts across the country.

Hoboken is located on the banks of the Hudson River.

Just across the Hudson River from Manhattan, Hoboken is the most walkable city in the US, according to Only in Your State’s most recent analysis. The one-square-mile-city earned a nearly perfect Walk Score of 97, thanks to its dense street grid, many mixed-use buildings, and abundance of public transportation. Three other cities in the Garden State also cracked the list’s top 10: Union City, West New York, and Jersey City.

Though LA isn’t known for being particularly pedestrian friendly, West Hollywood remains a walkable enclave.

Los Angeles County isn’t known for its public transit, but even so, West Hollywood snagged the fourth spot in Only in Your State’s analysis, earning a Walk Score of 91. The city packs over 300 restaurants, bars, and coffee shops into just under two square miles. West Hollywood’s walkability is especially prized because the area is one of LA’s most vibrant nightlife districts, creating safe accessibility for revelers on the world-famous Sunset Strip.

In addition to being one of the country’s oldest, Boston is also one of the most walkable cities.

“What makes Boston special is that every neighborhood has its own personality and you can experience so much of the city on foot,” says George Sarkis, CEO and cofounder of the Sarkis Team at Douglas Elliman. “You can walk from the historic brownstones of Beacon Hill to the boutiques and restaurants of Back Bay, through the Public Garden, and into the South End without ever feeling like you’re covering much ground.” Walking is the best way to truly enjoy all of the city’s historic sites, centuries-old architecture, and cobblestone streets.

San Francisco’s hilly landscape can turn a casual stroll into an all-in workout.

This Bay Area city has one of the highest transit scores in the country, making it a breeze to go car-free. But for many locals—like Wendy Newman, a realtor with Wesely & Associates and a resident since 1987—walking is the real experience. “​​In one outing, you can buy groceries, stop for coffee, admire a Victorian’s unreasonable paint palette, nod at the neighbor whose face you know but whose name you still haven’t learned, and discover a bookstore you weren’t looking for,” she says. Plus, San Francisco’s mild climate makes taking a spin through neighborhoods like Chinatown and North Beach enjoyable year round.

New York City is built on a grid system, making it easy to navigate.

Just about every expert we talked to mentioned NYC, a metropolis made for a good stroll. From the café-lined lanes of Little Italy to particularly scenic stretches like the East River Promenade and Brooklyn Bridge Park, the Big Apple is big on pedestrian access. And the city’s high walkability score has benefits beyond just saving on cab fare, points out Kate Wollman-Mahan, a real estate agent at Christie’s International Real Estate New York. “Life expectancy in New York City is five years longer than the national average, according to the city-run Health Dashboard and economist Jonathan Miller (82.3 years versus 77.3 years),” she says. “Walking is built into everyday life, from going out to coffee to running errands to walking in the park.”

A stroll around DC can take pedestrians past national monuments and charming townhouses.

According to Walk Score, a research platform that promotes walkability, DC has a walk score of 98. It's such a popular pedestrian playground that even local cultural institutions embed walking into their education approach. Take the Spy Museum, for example, which developed its own free walking tour because of the breadth of strolling space in the area and interest from both residents and tourists. “We send them around the National Mall and downtown area with espionage history that happened in those exact places throughout history,” says Aliza Bran, the museum’s director of media relations. Other particularly walkable neighborhoods include Dupont Circle, U Street, Capitol Hill, and Georgetown.

The Windy City is a certified walking city.

The Windy City tops the US ranking for walkability from Preply, a language learning marketplace that runs walkability studies across the US, Europe, and Asia. “You can walk between Chicago’s five most-visited attractions (the Art Institute, Millennium Park, Cloud Gate, the Chicago Architecture Center, and the Magnificent Mile) in 28 minutes, about 2,500 steps,” says Preply’s communications leader Madeline Enos.

Sheldon Bishop, the founder of BrewAtlas, a guide to independent specialty coffee shops, argues that the best sign of a walkable neighborhood is a good cafe. And Chicago is particularly well caffeinated, thanks to 95 specialty cafes across 41 neighborhoods. “When people can walk to get coffee, they run into neighbors, stop into the bookstore next door, and spend money on the block instead of at a drive-thru,” he says.

Miami is full of walkable pockets, according to local residents.

For Sandra Masis, director of sales at the residential building Arbor Coconut Grove, it’s neighborhoods like Coconut Grove that make Miami such a pedestrian-friendly city. “You can walk to restaurants, cafés, shops, parks, and the waterfront, all while enjoying the neighborhood’s lush greenery and relaxed atmosphere,” she says. “It has a sense of community that makes you want to get outside and explore.” And Miami’s newer developments, like Brickell, prioritize walkability as much as older neighborhoods like Coconut Grove and Little Havana. “Even in a city built around cars, you can create pockets where people can comfortably live, work, and socialize on foot,” says Masis.

Contemporary high-rises mingle with historic sites in Philadelphia.

Every year GuruWalk, a platform for free tours, puts together a ranking of the best cities in the world to explore on foot. In their most recent study, NYC took the top spot among US cities, and Philadelphia came in just behind it. GuruWalk’s Carolina Martínez says that though Philly gets fewer visitors than other places on the list, those who explore it love it. “Philadelphia has a 4.99 average rating, with 99 percent of reviews at five stars,” she says. “Walk a few blocks through the historic district and you’re standing in front of Independence Hall and the Liberty Bell.” New Orleans, San Francisco, and Boston round out the top five.

#4984. 9 Best Winter Boots for Women, Tested and Reviewed by Fashion Editors

After years of testing, our top pick is from Sorel.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

As a style writer, I live in comfortable sneakers and heels for most of the year. But come winter, my favorite shoes can’t handle New York City’s slick, slushy streets. That’s when I swap in a tried-and-true waterproof winter boot. For the past two years, WH editors have been testing tons of cold-weather shoes and have found the best options that keep our feet warm without skimping on style.

During evaluations, we put a range of comfortable boots to the test, including chic leather options for warmer climates, warm hiking boots, and insulated styles for the chilliest locales. We wore them during long, snowy days on Finland’s slopes and wet morning commutes to our New York City office. The result? A curated guide to the absolute best options for different preferences and weather conditions.

What’s New: - At the close of last winter, we added The North Face Thermoball Lace Up Waterproof Boots to our guide. Dorenna Newton, executive producer for Men’s Health and Women’s Health, tested this boot in St. Moritz while covering Team USA’s bobsled training ahead of the Olympics. - In our November 2026 update, I added the Columbia Snowtrot Mid Boot and the Moon Boot Icon Low Pearly Plus after testing both pairs in Iceland. Both scored major style points, while the Snowtrot excelled in wet conditions and the Moon Boot won me over with its extreme warmth and comfort when temps plummeted.- The Columbia Minx Shorty IV Boot continues to prove its top-notch water resistance and warmth. This boot won me over in Finland last year, and I wore it nonstop during winter 2026 in NYC while navigating slushy streets and freezing temps.- We’re currently testing a handful of more winter boots as temperatures start to drop and will update our guide with the best new styles we find.

We were first drawn to the Sorel Caribou boot because its laces and colorways look more stylish than most other winter boots. But after testing it for over two years, we found it’s also warm enough to handle super-cold temps and repels water like a pro.

Starting with style, the quilted padding, metal hardware, and lace-up front closure add a nice design touch that separates this from other serious winter boots. It pairs well with jeans, leggings, or even a cool maxi skirt or dress. We love the orange color, but if that shade is a little too much for you, the brand also makes beige and black styles that pair well with any outfit.

These boots also have lugged soles for excellent traction and are insulated and waterproof to keep you warm and dry in serious weather conditions. The upper is made from high-quality leather with a seam-sealed construction to keep moisture out. Plus, they have a rubber mudguard at the toes for extra warmth and protection, and a removable synthetic liner so you can customize your level of insulation.

“The fit is secure, so nothing is getting through these babies,” says Saladino, who wears this for snowy weekdays in New York City and ski weekends. “They’re perfect for après-skiing, shoveling the driveway, or anytime you need complete and total protection from the elements,” she says.

Her only complaint? They are a little on the heavier side, weighing in at two pounds and one ounce.

This cozy boot is the perfect pull-on style for casual snowy days and quick outings, Saladino says. Case in point: it's ideal when you need to take the dog out or you’re jetting out the door for school drop-off. There’s also extra room inside the boot, which Saladino loves when she needs to slip it on and go.

Despite the short style, the weatherproof suede boot is plenty warm. The faux-fur lining adds a cozy layer of insulation. We also like how light it is, even though it has a thick rubber outsole that’s super grippy on slippery surfaces.

Plus, it has a clean, simple design and cozy-chic look that you’ll actually want to wear all winter long. We love the contrast between the suede upper and the lighter-colored outsole, and think this pair looks adorable styled with a pair of wool socks and leggings.

Our only word of warning is that because the shaft height on this boot is on the shorter side, it may not be ideal for extra snowy days. If you need a boot to handle more intense winter weather, we recommend opting for The North Face Shellista IV Mid WP.

On an October trip to Iceland, I opted for this waterproof pair, since fall there is super unpredictable and rainy. While I opted for hiking boots most of the time, the Columbia Snowtrot Boot proved its waterproof capabilities on a particularly wet day of the trip, not letting a drop of water inside the shoe.

Considering how warm it kept my feet, I know I’ll be reaching for these all winter in New York. I love the puffy silhouette with its quilted upper and the monochrome finish in the all black colorway (which looks way more streamlined than similar pairs I’ve seen with white or gum soles). It’s lightweight, easy to pack, and has a sleek look that styles well with leggings and a puffer jacket or sweater. I especially like that they don't have the bulky feel I’ve come to expect from a winter boot.

Where this boot really stands out is its weather protection. It’s made with multiple layers of breathable synthetic materials and reinforced seams that help keep rain and moisture out. It also has 200 grams of insulation and a soft fleece interior to keep feet warm and cozy.

I also appreciate the side zipper, which makes them easy to slip on and off (especially handy when putting on your boots while bending over in bulky pants and jackets). The rubber outsole also helps provide tons of traction on both wet and dry surfaces, with a grippy tread pattern that comes in handy while navigating rainy sidewalks, muddy paths, or icy ground.

While it costs a fraction of the price of other winter boots, Dream Pairs makes a solid winter shoe that keeps cold and wet weather out. In addition to a water-resistant faux leather, the shoe has a plush faux-fur interior that’s super insulating. It also has a 1.37-inch rubber anti-slip sole for better traction in slippery conditions. The sole has 2mm of plush memory foam for added comfort and shock absorption. We also appreciate the pull tab on the back of the boot, which makes them easier to slip on and off.

Like our top overall pick, we like that this winter boot comes in a variety of colors: beige, nude, light gray, chestnut, and black. The cozy faux-fur lining, monochromatic laces, side panel stitching, and sturdy sole make it super stylish and practical. It easily slips under snow and ski pants, and looks just as cute styled with a pair of leggings and fuzzy socks.

On the downside, our testers said they noticed signs of wear and tear sooner than other pairs, particularly ripping of the faux-fur lining.

When I started crafting my packing list for a recent trip to Northern Finland, a cute but snow-proof winter boot was at the top of my list. After lots of research, I landed on the Columbia Minx Shorty IV Boot—a perfect blend of style, traction, water resistance, and warmth.

The moment I slipped this boot on, I loved the cozy, faux-fur insulation that kept my feet warm even on frigid mornings and evenings. And the waterproof nylon outer meant I didn’t have to worry about slushy streets or fresh snow seeping through. The traction is also super impressive, and I felt comfortable walking over icy sidewalks, a frozen lake, and snowy trails.

The shaft height hits about two inches above the ankle, offering just the right amount of coverage for typical city snow or moderate winter adventures. While deeper snow might call for a taller boot, this pair provides the ideal balance between protection and mobility.

The cherry on top? It’s genuinely so stylish. The sleek design, color selection, and faux-fur collar mean you don’t have to sacrifice fashion for function. I’ve paired them with everything from fleece-lined leggings and Fair Isle sweaters, snow pants and a winter coat, and even jeans—and they always look great. It was my go-to boot for the whole trip, and I know I’ll get just as much use out of it this winter in New York.

If your feet are perpetually cold once winter hits, meet your new favorite snow boots. The boot features an extremely thick layer of insulation in the form of a cushioned foam lining that molds to your feet over time. Wearing this boot genuinely feels like stepping into a giant marshmallow. I especially like that the platforms lift your feet off the cold, wet ground, which makes a huge difference in keeping them toasty. Plus, the shearling details add a cozy touch.

I also love Moon Boot’s iconic, space-age silhouette with its oversized shape. The Italian brand has built a reputation for seriously chic après-ski style, but don't let the fashion-forward appearance fool you. The rubber tread outsole provides plenty of grip on snowy surfaces, while the surprisingly lightweight feel makes them comfortable for everyday winter outings, whether you're heading to the lodge or walking around town after a fresh snowfall.

Fun fact: These boots are ambidextrous, meaning there's no designated left or right boot, and they mold to your feet as you wear them (though I will say they feel really good straight out of the box).

After testing this boot in Iceland, I can say that having a genuinely warm, insulated pair of boots makes a big difference when you're outside in the cold for extended periods. This pair is hands-down my favorite option for especially frigid locations when warmth is your biggest priority, particularly if you're looking for something that doesn't sacrifice style.

Just note that the upper is water-repellent rather than fully waterproof, so I'd save them for snowy conditions rather than slushy streets or rainy days.

This boot scores major style points, whether you’re on the mountain or navigating slushy city streets. We love the nude tone (with a subtle sheen), horizontal stitching, and puffy silhouette. Toggle laces keep the look neat and polished, while branded pull loops on both sides add functional visual interest.

Dorenna Newton, executive producer for Men’s Health and Women’s Health, recently wore this boot in St. Moritz while covering Team USA’s bobsled training ahead of the Olympics. She loved that they looked “super chic” for après adventures in the village—earning style points along with practicality.

“I’m constantly on the move, so packing light is non-negotiable,” Newton says. “That means every item has to earn its place—including this brand-new pair of The North Face snow boots I decided to test in real time.” She says this boot delivered from the moment she slipped it on, feeling instantly comfortable while providing serious warmth on frigid mountainsides.

The shaft hits right above the ankle and delivers impressive warmth thanks to recycled polyester insulation and a cozy fleece lining. A drawcord cinches the boot at the ankle for added protection from cold air, snow, and rain. Paired with a durable, waterproof ripstop upper made from recycled water bottles, the boot fares well under any condition.

Like the The North Face Shellista IV, this boot has exceptional traction thanks to a grippy, rubber outsole, which Newton says kept her steady on icy ground. However, like our other pick from The North Face, this boot has a snug, rigid fit—so consider sizing up if you’re between sizes or have a wider foot.

We’re big fans of L.L. Bean’s iconic Duck Boot, and this fleece version is perfect for chilly weather. “I love the rugged weekend styling of this boot,” says Saladino. “It’s super cozy on the inside, and the fleece detailing and contrasting laces on the outside are cool styling touches.”

It’s also well-equipped for slushy snow, thanks to a waterproof leather upper to keep moisture out. Plus, it has a rubber outsole with a grooved tread pattern that helps you stay upright and balanced on slick sidewalks. Bonus: The boot is made with 200 grams of PrimaLoft insulation, an ultra-warm, quick-drying material to keep feet dry and toasty.

The brand doesn’t have half sizes, but we found it wise to size down if you’re between sizes. “I am a size 9, and it fits great even with a thick sock,” says Saladino. Overall, we were impressed by both style and function—particularly the traction, which makes it a bit more equipped for heavy winter conditions than other ankle-length styles.

The Ugg Classic Mini Platform is a well-loved classic, and freelance stylist Alexandra Juric says they're constantly in rotation during the fall and winter. It’s stylish, comfortable, and warm. It has that classic Ugg boot shape but with a trendy platform that gives total cool-girl vibes. But you'd better hurry up and *add to cart* if you want this style ahead of the holidays—it’s known to sell out quickly.

The inside of the boot is lined with the brand’s signature wool blend, which adds a fluffy layer of insulation and comfort. It also has a heavily cushioned EVA foam midsole that feels super plush under the foot. The suede outer adds to the cozy look and feel, though it’s less water-resistant than other leathers. We recommend snagging the shoe protector spray if you plan to wear them in wet weather. We also love that the added two-inch platform to the classic silhouette gives you height without sacrificing comfort or traction.

Associate health and fitness editor Addison Aloian says this boot is the perfect addition to her winter wardrobe. "I've worn them on wet, rainy, snowy streets and they've stayed relatively clean, even in a lighter color," she says. "They're insanely comfortable with the plush interior, warm in even the coldest temps, and totally cradle my tired feet—I feel like I could walk miles in them!" She loves that they are super stylish and go with just about any outfit, but she's partial to styling them with a long trouser or flared leggings.

The downside? They don’t come in half sizes and tend to run big, so keep that in mind when shopping.

The type of boot you want for everyday city commuting is different from what you'd need for winter hiking or snowshoeing in seriously cold weather, says Julie Hardy, senior buyer at Zappos.

While a boot’s temperature ratings provide a useful starting point for what to look for when shopping, there's no one-size-fits-all rule, says Hardy. For most outdoor activities like snowshoeing, hiking, or backpacking, our experts suggest looking for a baseline of about 200 grams of insulation. Liners can also boost the product's warmth. Seam-sealed and waterproof features are also important if you spend a good amount of time in the snow or rain. Look for terms like "Thinsulate" and "GORE-TEX," both of which are trademarked materials that provide superior insulation.

Keep in mind that taller boots also help keep more of your leg warm even without the presence of snow. Generally, mid-height boots (about 6.5 inches) strike the perfect balance of coverage and comfort; however, tall heights (12 inches or more) are better suited for deep snow. Ultimately, shaft height is about personal preference, but make sure your boots go above the hem of your pants to keep snow and cold air out.

As a fashion writer who’s tried countless pairs of shoes over the past ten years, I know a thing or two about what makes a solid winter boot. I went to school in chilly upstate New York for four years, have a lifetime of NYC winters under my belt, and regularly travel to places with extreme conditions (most recently, Iceland and Finnish Lapland—above the Arctic Circle).

We first wrote this article in November 2023 with our team’s all-time favorite winter boots. But since we’re always testing new pairs each winter and during cold-weather vacations, I’m constantly updating the list with the absolute best winter boots from brands like Sorel, Columbia, Moon Boot, The North Face, Merrell, Ugg, and L.L. Bean.

I also consulted a senior buyer for Zappos, which is one of the largest footwear retailers out there, on what to consider when shopping for winter boots: materials, insulation, and shaft height. WH fashion contributors Kristin Saladino, Rose Lauture, Lily Wohlner, and freelance stylist Alexandra Juric also weighed in on their favorite options. Collectively, we have over 30 years of experience testing shoes and boots.

#4983. Jensen Huang Resigns as Nvidia CEO, With CFO Colette Kress Named Interim Chief

Jensen Huang Resigns as Nvidia CEO, With CFO Colette Kress Named Interim Chief

Jensen Huang has resigned as CEO of Nvidia, ending a 33-year tenure at the helm of the chipmaker he co-founded, after reports of affairs with a suspected Chinese spy and a senior company executive.

Huang has stepped down as president, CEO and a member of the board, effective immediately, Nvidia said on Thursday. Colette Kress, Nvidia's chief financial officer since 2013, will serve as interim CEO while remaining CFO, as the board searches for a permanent successor from both inside and outside the company. Nvidia did not give a reason for Huang's resignation, but thanked him for his "immeasurable contributions to the company and the industry."

NVIDIA announces CEO transition. Read the release: https://t.co/MTTUvFHCJA

— NVIDIA Newsroom (@nvidianewsroom) November 12, 2026

The board also named Harvey Jones, a former CEO and executive chairman of chip design software company Synopsys, as its first-ever chair.

"The Board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the company's operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption," Jones said.

Kress said she was "committed to building on the extraordinary foundation Jensen created over the last 33 years." Nvidia will report its third-quarter results as scheduled on Tuesday, the company said.

Nvidia shares were down 7.7% at $160.28 shortly after the market opened on Thursday, having fallen as low as $148.80, according to Yahoo! Finance data.

Huang's departure comes four days after he acknowledged in a letter to staff that he had been unfaithful to his wife, prompting Nvidia's board to open an independent investigation. He had told the board that he had been intimate with Alice Huang, an executive at Singapore-based Nvidia customer Megaspeed, The New York Times reported.

The Times had reported in October that U.S. officials had concluded Alice Huang was a Chinese intelligence asset, while the Financial Times later reported that a U.S. government memo said the two had been romantically involved. China has denied the allegations.

On Wednesday, the Times reported that Huang had also had a relationship with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of health care, who reports to him. The Wall Street Journal later reported that the board was preparing to oust him, adding to the pressure on the CEO.

The scandal has wiped hundreds of billions of dollars off Nvidia's market value, with the company losing about $430 billion in two days earlier this week. Nvidia and Huang also face a proposed shareholder class action, and lawmakers had threatened to subpoena him.

Huang ran Nvidia with about 60 executives reporting directly to him and has said he did not believe in succession planning, leaving the company without an obvious successor.

Prior to the announcement of Huang's resignation, one person inside the company had told the Financial Times that naming Kress as interim CEO would be the simplest option. Other potential candidates cited by the paper's sources include Ian Buck, who runs Nvidia's data center business, and Arm CEO Rene Haas, a former Nvidia executive.

Huang remains Nvidia's largest shareholder, with a stake of about 3.6%, according to the Journal.

#4982. Inside the Governor’s Mansions That Will Soon Be Home to 19 Newly Elected Leaders

In January, the United States’ 19 newly elected governors won’t just have inauguration day on their calendars, but moving day, too. All but five states have governor’s mansions for their leaders to live and work from, with the exceptions being Arizona, Idaho, Massachusetts, Rhode Island, and Vermont. These executive residences tend to be grand in style and scale, often possessing a long, rich history—even some haunted ones.

Sometimes, governors forego their official residences in favor of privately owned homes. For example, California governor Gavin Newsom recently listed the $7.5 million Fair Oaks home he lived in instead of the official executive residence, a 19th-century Victorian in Sacramento. Montana’s governor’s mansion is currently empty and in disrepair, while a governor hasn’t inhabited the official residence in Nebraska in over a decade.

Most states, however, have longstanding traditions of their leaders living in their official governor’s mansions. Virginia has the oldest continuously inhabited executive residence—every governor has lived there since 1813. If our new league of governors opts for tradition, here’s where they will be resting their heads for the next few years.

Republican Tommy Tuberville won Alabama’s gubernatorial race and will be able to move into a 119-year-old mansion in Montgomery following his inauguration. Built in 1907 by architect T. Weatherly Carter, the 17-room neoclassical residence features a row of four Corinthian columns at its front entrance, which opens to a foyer with a grand bifurcated staircase.

California’s new governor-elect is democrat Xavier Becerra, who will soon reside at a three-story Victorian in Sacramento. Built in 1877, the 20,000-square-foot house was thoroughly renovated in 2015, decades after Ronald and Nancy Reagan’s brief stint living there in 1967 before deeming it uninhabitable. The mansion was then used as a museum until its 2015 update, when it became home to the state’s governors once again. It has 30 rooms, Italian marble fireplaces, original wood floors, and a kidney-shaped swimming pool.

In Denver, democrat Phil Weiser will receive the keys to the red brick governor’s mansion known as Colorado’s Home. Built in 1908, it has been the state’s official governor’s residence since 1960. The Colonial Revival–style home has 27 rooms and shares its one-acre grounds with gardens, a historic gazebo, a greenhouse, and a carriage house. Thanks to John Hickenlooper, governor from 2011 to 2019, the home also has a rotating selection of local beers on tap.

Republican Byron Donalds just made history as Florida’s first Black governor. The state’s Greek Revival–style governor’s mansion in Tallahassee was completed in 1956 on the site of the original governor’s mansion built in 1907, which had fallen into disrepair. A screened swimming pool, a greenhouse, and a rose garden are among its amenities.

Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms has the distinction of being the first Black woman governor-elect in the United States. Her soon-to-be residence in Atlanta is a 30-room Greek Revival mansion that was built in 1967 by architect A. Thomas Bradbury. Across three levels, it measures in at 30,000 square feet.

Iowa’s governor’s mansion, known as Terrace Hill, will soon be home to democrat Rob Sand. Dating all the way back to 1869, Terrace Hill is an 18,000-square-foot Second Empire–style residence in Des Moines where Iowa’s governors have lived since 1976. Its first two floors are open to the public as a museum and events space.

Democrat Hannah Pingree will move into the Blaine House in Augusta, Maine. Originally built in 1833 in a combination of Federal and Greek Revival styles, the mansion has been the state’s official governor’s residence since 1919, when renovations gave it a more Colonial style. It has 28 rooms across two stories, including a billiards room. The property also hosts a formal garden and a tennis court.

In Michigan, democrat Jocelyn Benson has the good fortune of governing a state with two official executive residences: one in the state’s capital of Lansing, and an official summer residence on Mackinac Island. The former is a 1957-built 8,700-square-foot ranch-style home with five bedrooms and four bathrooms; the latter is an Arts-and-Crafts-style mansion that was built in 1901.

Democrat Amy Klobuchar, currently a US senator for Minnesota, won her state’s gubernatorial election and will move into a 16,000-square-foot residence in St. Paul that dates to 1912. It features seven bedrooms, 10 bathrooms, and nine fireplaces. Architect William Channing Whitney designed the stone-and-brick dwelling in a combination of English Tudor and Beaux-Arts styles.

Nevada also elected its first Black governor, democrat Aaron Ford, who will soon reside in a Classical Revival–style mansion designed by architect George A. Ferris in 1909. It features a grand entrance hall, a reception room, a formal dining room, a governor’s study, and a luncheon room, in addition to private living quarters.

New Mexico’s governor-elect, democrat Deb Haaland, has made history as the first Native American woman to hold that title. In Santa Fe, the governor’s mansion is referred to as The People’s House. Designed by architect W.C. Kruger, the midcentury-modern structure was completed in 1955.

Democrat Amy Acton will be Ohio’s next governor, granting her residency inside a 1925 mansion in Bexley, just outside of Columbus. The Tudor-style home was built by architect Robert Gilmore Hanford, and its grounds boast three acres of extensive gardens, including a “Storybook Trail” inspired by The Tales of Peter Rabbit.

Republican governor-elect Mike Mazzei will be the new steward of Oklahoma’s official governor’s residence in Tulsa. The 14,000-square-foot Dutch Colonial was built in 1928 and has a limestone façade that matches the State Capitol building two blocks away. Uniquely, its grounds boast a swimming pool shaped like Oklahoma, an outdoor kitchen, and a tennis court on the former site of a helipad where President Lyndon B. Johnson landed in 1964.

Helena Foulkes, a democrat, won the race for Rhode Island’s governor, but she isn’t necessarily going to move out of her current residence any time soon, since the state doesn’t have an official governor’s mansion. Foulkes owns two homes in the state: a $3.8 million eight-bedroom summer house in Narragansett and a $2.3 million three-bedroom home in Providence.

Republican governor-elect Alan Wilson won South Carolina’s vote, making his next home a two-story Federal-style mansion dating back to 1855. Originally used as military academy officers’ quarters, it has been the official governor’s residence since 1868, three years after the Civil War ended. Inside, it has a central entrance hall connecting to two drawing rooms, a state dining room, and a library. Private living quarters are located on the second floor.

Marsha Blackburn, the Republican governor-elect of Tennessee, can move to a 10-acre estate in Nashville’s Oak Hill suburb. Known as Far Hills for the surrounding picturesque views, the three-story Georgian-style mansion was built in 1931. It has been the state’s official governor’s residence since 1949.

In Texas, democrat Gina Hinojosa will be the first Latina to hold the state’s highest office. Her official residence will be a Greek Revival–style mansion in Austin. Spanning nearly 10,000 square feet, it has been home to Texas’s governors since it was built in 1856.

Democrat David Crowley won in Wisconsin, making him the first Black governor of the state. The state’s executive residence is a Classical Revival–style mansion located in Madison. Completed in 1928, it has 16,000 square feet of interior living space across three floors, including seven bedrooms, 13 bathrooms, seven fireplaces, and 10 gardens.

Republican Eric Barlow will soon have access to the governor’s residence near Frontier Park in Cheyenne, Wyoming. In 1976, it replaced the state’s former executive residence, which was built in 1905 and is now open as a museum. Available details of the current mansion are sparse, however, and it seems that a new, upgraded version with improved security and functionality may soon be in the works.

#4980. Are Alt-L1s over? Security, costs drive smaller chains to Ethereum

L1 blockchains Zilliqa, Gnosis and Ronin join the Ethereum ecosystem as the costs of going it alone grow, and the security and liquidity advantages become too hard to resist.

Running an independent blockchain has always come with a particular promise: control.

A standalone Layer 1 architecture allows projects to be the masters of their own destiny, set the rules of consensus, maintain their own validators and build infrastructure around their users, without relying on any other network.

But with great power comes great responsibility — and considerable expense.

Blockchains need enough users, developers and economic activity to support the infrastructure that secures them. For some smaller networks, keeping an entire ecosystem afloat no longer makes economic sense.

Zilliqa is the latest project to confront that reality, announcing plans last week to move away from its L1 architecture and become an Ethereum-based Layer 2 (L2) instead:

“Usage never reached what a standalone chain needs,” the post explained. “Funding and liquidity for standalone L1s dried up. The recent hacks showed how much it takes to secure a chain alone. The data is onchain for everyone to see.”

Blockchain ecosystem Gnosis (which has always been Ethereum-adjacent) is also working to transition Gnosis Chain into an Ethereum-based network through its Ethereum Economic Zone (EEZ) initiative. Its RollupX test network intends to show how participating chains can share Ethereum settlement and synchronous composability as native rollups.

Gaming-focused Ronin blockchain also completed its move back to Ethereum as an L2 in May.

With interest in alt L1s drying up along with the funding, will we start to see more and more chains consolidating in the Ethereum ecosystem?

Independent L1s generally have to keep several core functions going, including execution, consensus, data availability and settlement. They must also fund the token incentives used to secure their networks through staking.

Related: What are native rollups? Full guide to Ethereum’s latest innovation

“It comes down to a chain’s profitability,” Max Shannon, senior research analyst at Bitwise, tells Magazine.

ZIL becomes a Layer 2 on Ethereum. Source: Zilliqa

“L2s only need to maintain execution and do not necessarily need a token, which makes them a more attractive financial and operational proposition,” he says.

Shannon says the top ten largest projects are more financially sustainable. Coinbase and Robinhood’s L2 networks generated $48 million and $52 million in fees respectively over the last 365 days, with margins above 90%. The next eight largest chains each generated more than $1 million annually.

From 11th place onward, however, annual fees collectively amounted to less than $1 million. So a network may have ambitious technology and a dedicated community, but those things don’t necessarily pay the bills.

Ethereum is often criticized for giving away too much of its revenue to L2s, but that’s precisely what makes it attractive to set up projects there.

But just becoming an Ethereum L2 doesn’t automatically make a network profitable however. Shannon points to Optimism, Katana and Linea as examples of networks whose expenses outweigh their fee revenue. Projects like Blast have already been forced to shut down.

Gnosis has targeted spring 2027 to complete its transition, which will see it give up its independent validator-based consensus in favor of Ethereum settlement.

Gnosis Chain will relaunch as an Ethereum Economic Zone. Source: Gnosis

“Two years ago I gave a talk about ‘native Rollups’ that would truly extend Ethereum instead of being isolated chains,” co-founder Martin Köppelmann said, adding that RollupX “even goes beyond what I had imagined back then!”

Gnosis’s EEZ initiative aims to enable separate Ethereum-based networks interact synchronously, so a smart contract on one network can call another and use its response within the same transaction, without relying on the usual bridge-and-wait process.

That’s a prospect that has some Ethereum supporters particularly bullish, with ETH bull materkel describing the EEZ as a potential “black hole for liquidity,” and saying it could allow participating networks to tap into Ethereum’s total value locked (TVL) and strengthen its position as a liquidity hub.

Could network effects, and increased interoperability draw more and more chains into Ethereum’s orbit?

It isn’t only the operating cost economics that make sense. Projects can also tap into an established ecosystem of applications and developers rather than building those network effects from scratch.

Alex Gluchowski, co-founder and chief executive of Matter Labs, the company behind Ethereum L2 ZKsync, says he expects more L1 teams to maintain their own chains while using Ethereum for settlement.

“Running an independent L1 means maintaining its security as well as building something people want to use,” Gluchowski tells Magazine.

But Gluchowski says sovereignty and Ethereum integration are not mutually exclusive, since chains can retain control over their rules and operations while using Ethereum for shared settlement.

Ronin completed migration to Ethereum in May. Source: Ronin

And for applications handling sensitive financial information, maintaining a private environment under their own control can be essential, he says. Shared settlement does not have to mean exposing confidential transaction data.

The biggest obstacle today is that all the L2s are effectively isolated from each other and from Ethereum. It’s certainly possible to jump between them, but its far from seamless and interoperability is poor.

Shannon cautions against describing the market as undergoing straightforward consolidation around Ethereum.

Some large institutions are launching L2 networks, he says, including Coinbase and Robinhood, but others are still choosing to build independent L1s, like Circle’s Arc, Stripe’s Tempo, or Hyperliquid’s purpose-specific perpetual futures blockchain.

“So there is no clear conclusion to the L1 versus L2 debate as yet,” he says.

Magazine: ‘Bunker mode’ won’t protect your crypto, critics say

All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy.

#4979. Rose Lavelle Suits Up to Build Gotham FC’s Future

Committed to the reigning NWSL champions for three more seasons, the soccer star reflects on leadership, life lessons, and a monumental move to a shiny new stadium in New York City.

Rose Lavelle is breaking down her outfit of the day, though it’s not the ensemble you’d expect from a professional athlete’s social media feed.

“My hard hat, which is… something,” the Gotham FC midfielder says with a chuckle. She looks down to survey the rest of her mandatory construction-site-chic attire. “My gloves—gardener’s. My boots—too big. My vest—cool.”

It’s a Tuesday afternoon in September, and Rose is the first of her teammates to tour the unfinished Etihad Park in Queens, New York, the stadium that Gotham will call home in 2028. Earlier that day, Gotham announced that Rose had signed a contract extension through 2029. In other words, she'll be back when this concrete floor is an actual soccer pitch.

We weave past steel beams and through what will eventually become Gotham's tunnel to the pitch. From there, the grandstands rise around us, a preview of the 25,000-seat venue that will soon welcome Gotham's fans. Rose poses for photos, holding a jersey that says “2029.”

A few days later, Gotham’s president of soccer operations, Yael Averbuch West, tells me she smiled when she saw the images of Rose in her hard hat. The look may be a departure from her usual uniform, but Averbuch West sees something fitting about it. As Gotham prepares to make another postseason run in pursuit of defending their 2025 National Women’s Soccer League title, Rose has become central to the club's plans.

“She has been part of literally constructing this vision with us,” Averbuch West says. “She has been an instrumental part of the vision of the club and our aspirations and our ambition.”

The roughly $780 million Etihad Park is being developed by Major League Soccer's New York City FC, which will share the stadium with Gotham beginning in 2028. It's a significant upgrade from Gotham's current arrangement at Sports Illustrated Stadium in Harrison, New Jersey, where the team uses the visitors' locker room and covers up the New York Red Bulls’ logos with its own banners. At Etihad Park, Gotham will have a locker room of its own, merchandise space in the team store, and digital signage and video boards that can be fully branded for Gotham on match days—no covering up another team's logos required.

The move is another sign of just how much women's professional soccer has changed since Rose entered the NWSL nearly a decade ago. And in that time, Rose has changed, too. At 31, the once up-and-coming star has become a veteran helping set the standard for a club that has no plans to slow down.

The NWSL is not the same league it was when Rose was drafted first overall by the now-defunct Boston Breakers in 2017, playing home games at a 2,500-seat facility at Harvard University. When I sit down with Rose after our stadium tour, she tells me it’s difficult to compare the things that mattered to her as a rookie to signing a contract extension now. “I think of the amount of support staff we had, it was so few—how many people were dedicated towards ticketing and marketing and all of that,” she says. “I notice that stuff a lot more now than I probably did at the time, because you don't know what you don't know.”

Rose has come a long way since then. She won the 2019 World Cup with the U.S. Women’s National Team, earned Olympic gold in Paris in 2024, and navigated injuries and moves between NWSL clubs. She joined Gotham in 2024 and, after ankle surgery sidelined her for the start of her second season, returned to help the club win the 2025 NWSL championship, scoring the winning goal. In January, she was named U.S. Soccer's 2025 Female Player of the Year.

On the field, Rose’s influence isn't always captured in goals or assists. Gotham rookie Andrea Kitahata calls her the club’s “quiet momentum-builder and tone-setter” for how she initiates the team's defensive press and creates attacking opportunities that may not show up on a stat sheet.

Rose has learned plenty from USWNT veterans like Megan Rapinoe, Becky Sauerbrunn, Alex Morgan, Christen Press, and Tobin Heath, who showed her that there was no single way to command respect in a locker room. “Everybody led in their own way,” Rose says. “Everybody's leadership style might have looked different, but it wasn't any more or less impactful than the other.”

Now, the younger players in Gotham’s locker room are learning from Rose—especially since her 2025 ankle surgery, the first of her career, which forced her to find new ways to contribute off the field.

She initially thought surgery would be a straightforward fix. “I think I thought you get surgery to fix it, and when you're off the table, you’re fixed, and it's smooth sailing. And it was not smooth sailing at all,” she says. She pauses. “Yeah, it was really painful! It was really painful.”

The physical recovery was only part of the challenge. Rose says the mental toll of rehabbing—and the uncertainty that came with it—tested her in ways she hadn't anticipated. “It taught me I had a whole ’nother level of mental grind and fortitude that I could battle through,” she says. “I think you learn the most about yourself in the tough moments. I mean, it was hard—but I made it through, and I feel like I gained so much.”

Rose has never been the loudest voice in the locker room, but recent years have taught her that she doesn’t need to be.

Ahead of the 2025 season, Rose told Averbuch West that she would be more intentional about creating the change she wanted to see within the club. “When you're Rose Lavelle, you are a leader, whether you choose to be or not,” Averbuch West says. “It doesn't have to come in big speeches in front of the group. It’s not going to be that. But it's the little things she does behind the scenes, the little connections with players—pulling someone aside, talking on the field.”

Her teammates take cues from her, Averbuch West says. If Gotham is down a goal at halftime, they look to Rose to gauge how she's responding. If Rose is inspired, they’re inspired. And Rose has become increasingly intentional about the example she sets. “She's been very proactive during her time here to share really constructive feedback with us, and to also be part of creating a locker room culture that I think the players at this point are really enjoying,” Averbuch West says.

“Obviously, we had some cheaters in there,” Kitahata jokes. “Rose made sure that she held everyone accountable, made sure no one's looking it up on their phone.”

And Rose isn’t exactly a quiet participant. “She's in the meal room screaming out,” Kitahata recalls, imitating Rose’s clues: “Blank, blank, blank, blank. It's four words. Figure it out, guys!”

She’s equally serious about her teammates’ recovery. During a July road trip to Portland, Rose took Kitahata, still navigating her first professional season, along for a day of mental and physical recovery. They got coffee, browsed the shelves at Powell's Books, and headed to a sauna and cold plunge. A stickler for her recovery routine, Rose insisted they stay submerged up to their necks in the icy water for four minutes. “I'm like, ‘Rose. Rose, I'm freezing!’” Kitahata recalls. But Rose wouldn't budge. “I love that she always holds me accountable for my recovery.” They finished the day with sushi.

Rose is the teammate you want to run into when you’re having a bad day, because she notices, Kitahata says. “Even with a look, she's like, ‘Oh my god, what's wrong? Do we need to go get coffee?’ … It’s very rare to find someone of her stardom that is just so willing to give to the team.”

Rose has become more conscious of how much she'll miss these moments someday. “I'm such a painfully nostalgic person,” she says. “It's like, debilitating sometimes, honestly.”

It's the everyday moments with her teammates that seem to stick with her most. “I was thinking the other day, having moments in the locker room where we have this shared playlist that we're playing, and we're all dancing to it. And I was like, ‘How many grown adults get to dance with their friends in the locker room?’” she says. “It’s little things like that that it's so easy to take for granted, because we get to do it literally every single day, but something that I think is just so special.”

It's also clear how much she values the life she's built at Gotham, and how much she wants to keep being part of it. “I love my life off-field. I love my life on-field. I think we have such an incredible group of players and staff that we get to work with and learn from each day,” she says. “I just felt so content, but also challenged in a way that I felt like I was going to be happily growing.”

For Gotham, the move to Queens isn't just about having a place to call its own. It's also an opportunity to reach a bigger audience. Despite winning two NWSL championships in three seasons, Gotham has struggled to fill the stands in New Jersey consistently. But when the club hosted the Washington Spirit at Citi Field, home of the New York Mets, for its Queens Classic in July, the sellout crowd of 42,175 offered a glimpse of what might be possible across the street at Etihad Park.

The move isn't without its complications. Gotham has deep roots in New Jersey, and some longtime supporters are disappointed to see the club leave. “I was a little sad at first that the team was moving,” says Lindsey Smith, 37, a New Jersey resident and five-year season ticket holder. “But this is so much bigger than a location to me. As someone who wants to make women's sports accessible, I'm very excited for Gotham to move to Queens.”

Smith’s friend Krista Tonnesen, 37, has been attending Gotham matches regularly since 2021. An Astoria resident, that has meant long trips home after night games, sometimes with 40-minute waits between PATH trains. She's thrilled to have Gotham in her borough, and hopeful that the shorter trip will bring more fans into the stands. “The players and the team deserve better,” she says. “I really think moving to New York will turn that around.”

Rose says the atmosphere at the Queens Classic, where she scored the game's only goal, left an impression on her. “To think that we could be playing in front of a crowd and atmosphere like that, every single home game, I think it'll just add that much more to what we're building,” she says.

Back at Etihad Park, as we make our way through the unfinished concrete hallways, I ask Rose if she can picture what it'll be like when Gotham finally takes the field here. “I see the vision,” she says.

She also leads the sports and athletes vertical, traveling to cover the Paris Olympics, Women’s World Cup, WNBA Finals, and NCAA Final Four for WH. She has nearly 15 years of experience writing, editing, and managing social media for national and international publications and is also a NASM-certified personal trainer. A proud native of Pittsburgh, PA, she is a graduate of Ohio University’s E.W. Scripps School of Journalism. Follow her on Instagram @alucci.

#4978. The Term ‘Medical-Grade Skincare’ is Confusing. Here’s What You Need to Know.

Is it the key to a healthier complexion or just a gimmick? Experts weigh in.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Somewhere between the serum shelf and your For You page, “medical-grade” became the most reassuring phrase in beauty. It conjures authoritative images of a lab coat, a prescription pad, and the idea that your money is going somewhere serious. But, plot twist: Not one person regulates the term. “The phrase ‘medical-grade’ is vague and arbitrary,” dermatologist Joshua Zeichner, MD, says bluntly. “It does not correlate with effectiveness or safety.”

And consumers are starting to catch on. After all, there are only a few skincare ingredients proven to work, and dermatologist Mona Gohara, MD, says skeptical shoppers ask her about the term every day. “My patients and followers are wondering, ‘is this skincare backed by science, and will it work?’ They want to know whether they’re investing in healthier skin or just a prettier bottle,” she says. After all, no one wants to be duped by products and ingredients that are a total scam.

Meet the experts: Joshua Zeichner, MD, is an associate professor of dermatology and the director of cosmetic & clinical research in dermatology at Mount Sinai Hospital in New York City. Mona Gohara, MD, is a clinical professor in the department of dermatology at Yale School of Medicine.

So, let’s sort the science from the sales pitch. Read on to find out what the term (kinda, sorta) means, what it doesn’t, and how to shop the skincare aisle like a dermatologist.

Ask around, and the definitions of ‘”medical-grade” skincare multiply. Dr. Zeichner says it usually indicates a product with a high concentration of science-backed active ingredients, but not always. Depending on the brand, it can refer to the quality of a product’s ingredients, any clinical testing on the final formula, or the use of one particular active.

Dr. Gohara likens the phrase to physician-dispensed skincare designed to complement dermatologic care (in other words, the products you’d purchase at a doctor’s office). But now, she says it became shorthand for formulas built around specific skin concerns and that its visibility has grown as consumers have become more interested in the ingredients and science behind their routines.

Shoppers tend to assume these products are stronger and come with a dermatologist’s guidance, but they shouldn’t, says Dr. Zeichner. In his view, the skincare market is extremely crowded, and this is a way brands are chasing a competitive edge. In some cases, the term is used interchangeably with other unregulated phrases, like “dermatologist-formulated” or “dermatologist-approved,” giving the appearance of a professional quality product, whatever that means.

Let’s start with the regulatory facts. “Medical-grade” is not an FDA-recognized term, Dr. Gohara confirms. In fact, the agency only very loosely regulates the claims of cosmetics. They do draw a line between a cosmetic product and a pharmaceutical (like prescription tretinoin, for example): Cosmetics, which include most moisturizers and serums, are meant to improve the appearance of skin. They can’t claim to change skin’s structure or function.

Over-the-counter drugs, such as acne treatments, sunscreens, and eczema treatments, contain active ingredients cleared by the FDA for a specific condition and their packaging includes a drug facts box listing the active ingredients. This part’s important: The term “medical-grade” does not indicate that a product is an OTC drug. “Slapping it on a label doesn’t give a product a separate regulatory status,” says Dr. Gohara.

The bottom line is that “if you see the term on a label, take it with a big grain of salt,” advises Dr. Zeichner. He says the definition “can mean whatever the brand wants it to.” While the products may have higher concentrations of actives, there are no specific percentages shoppers can look for. Dr. Gohara says that many physician-dispensed brands invest in clinical studies, ingredient stability, and delivery systems that help actives work while limiting irritation, but you can’t always tell from the label alone. “The value comes from that research, formulation, and guidance,” she says. “The term alone doesn’t tell you which advantages a product offers.”

With packaging out of the picture, both doctors point to the same strategy: Start with what your skin needs, then pick ingredients with a proven track record of addressing those concerns. To that end, Dr. Zeichner says retinol is perhaps the best-studied ingredient for aging skin. It’s a form of vitamin A shown to speed cell turnover and stimulate collagen.

He also singles out vitamin C as one to look for. It’s an antioxidant that helps neutralize free radical damage from UV, blocks abnormal pigment production to fade dark spots, and supports collagen production. Lastly, hydroxy acids such as alpha hydroxy acids (think glycolic and lactic) and beta hydroxy acids (salicylic acid) are also worth your dough. The former help dissolve the connections between surface cells so they shed more easily; the latter clears excess oil and helps keep pores clear.

Dr. Gohara names broad-spectrum sunscreen with an SPF 30 or higher as a must-have, a 5% niacinamide serum for uneven tone, and 2.5% benzoyl peroxide for inflamed acne, which she says can work at higher strengths as well (though irritation can happen). She adds that percentages aren’t interchangeable between ingredients, and that the finished formula and consistent use matter, too. If the box doesn’t list ingredient concentrations, Dr. Zeichner suggests snapping a photo of the box and researching the product at home before buying (brand websites often hold a lot of useful information).

And watch for red flags—Dr. Zeichner calls out vague language, a lack of transparency about ingredients or clinical testing, and promises that seem too good to be true. Dr. Gohara says claims to “erase” deep wrinkles, “eliminate” pores, or “clear” years of pigmentation in a weekend are warning signs. “If the promise sounds like a facelift by Friday, skip it.”

SkinBetter Science Alpha Ret Overnight Cream

A dermatologist favorite, Skin Better Science’s famed retinol pairs the vitamin A derivative with lactic acid and hydrators like squalane and ceramides to tackle wrinkles and skin texture with no irritation.

This serum, which is often duplicated, is widely-considered to be the best-of-the-best when it comes to vitamin C products. Pairing the ingredient with ferulic acid and vitamin E (where the product gets its name) keeps the C stable and boosts its effectiveness.

SkinMedica Even & Correct Brightening Treatment Pads

These days, there are more exfoliating pads lining store shelves than there are short-shorts in Harry Styles’ closet. SkinMedica’s are the best—effective, gentle, efficient.

Alastin Skincare HydraTint Pro Mineral Sunscreen SPF 36

Yes, you want an SPF that protects skin from damaging UVA/UVB rays. But this one does so much more: Ectoin shields you from visible blue light while a powerful antioxidant blend keeps free radical damage at bay. This the Medieval armor of your skincare routine.

La Roche-Posay Effaclar Duo Dual Action Acne Cleanser

When it comes to battling breakouts, benzoyl peroxide can’t be beaten. The prob: It can be irritating. A wash is a great solution, killing acne-causing bacteria without being too harsh on skin.

Revision Skincare Advanced Pigment Corrector+

These days, niacinamide serums are a dime a dozen. What makes Revision’s product worth its price is that it pairs this proven pigment fighter with advanced exosomal technology that helps boost skin longevity to stay healthier longer.

So what should your strategy be when shopping for skincare? A few general guidelines:

At the end of the day, the next time you see “medical-grade” on a label, consider it mere marketing copy and keep reading. What’s inside the jar and what the brand can show about the finished product is what matters.

It’s also worth noting that spending more doesn’t buy better results, and more products can mean more problems, advises Dr. Zeichner. Higher concentrations may be necessary in some cases and just cause irritation in others. He keeps regimens simple, with the morning devoted to protection and the evening to hydration and repair. He recommends two products in the morning and two at night for patients. Combining too many can make them less effective and may cause dryness or irritation.

Dr. Gohara also advises saving where you can. An affordable cleanser, moisturizer, and broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher make an excellent foundation. Spending more can be worthwhile for a targeted treatment with research behind the finished formula, such as a stabilized vitamin C serum, a well-formulated retinoid, or a peptide product with evidence for its specific claims. You might also be paying for technology that keeps ingredients stable, makes them easier to tolerate, or gives a more elegant texture. “The upgrade should serve your skin, not just your bathroom shelf,” she says.

Last, know when to skip the shopping. Dr. Gohara says to see a dermatologist for persistent acne, rashes, stubborn pigmentation, or repeated irritation, and promptly for a changing or bleeding spot.

Sometimes expert guidance is the best investment in the routine.

Amber is a graduate of Boston University and lives in Rye, New York.

Brian Underwood is beauty director at Women’s Health, where he oversees content strategy for the brand across all platforms, including digital, print, and social. Underwood previously served as beauty and wellness director at Oprah Daily and O, The Oprah Magazine. #4976. 12 Best Bar Carts to Elevate Your Entertaining Game (2026)

Bar carts are not just for someone who likes to host a cocktail soirée. In fact, the predecessor of the bar cart was once a Victorian-era tea trolley. Only when the Prohibition hit did these side tables on wheels find their true calling. Many, many iterations later—think: Alvar Aalto’s iconic tea trolley, which has inspired many picks on this list, and USM’s blessedly minimalist spin on the genre—the beauty of this booze trolley remains that it can completely transform a room or give a useless nook some purpose. Though they’ve fallen out of favor in many modern homes, these rolling wonders make an elegant—and functional—home base for all your mixology essentials, from stemware and decanters to liquor bottles and mixers. They also don’t have to be used to haul booze at all, instead doubling as portable storage for crafts, books, beauty or bathroom supplies, and houseplants.

Employed outside, a stylish bar cart can give a dinner party some polish or emulate a poolside butler. Load it up with a pitcher of lemonade and a basket of sunscreen on top, and extra towels on the bottom and you’ll never have to leave your lounge.

Made of solid and veneered walnut with elegant brass details, the Cotu (from the makers of our favorite bar cabinets) is a delightful midcentury-style bar cart that has a distinctive asymmetrical incline on each shelf. Use the wider edge for housing smaller utensils and glasses, while leaving the shallower end for larger bottles that are less likely to topple over the edge.

Birch Lane’s Reinhart cart speaks to farmhouse design, especially in this punchy shade of blue. Turned legs give the overall look a polished appearance, but scalloped paneling on the top shelf and a fun hue ground the piece in something that’s suitable for more playful design aesthetes.

Think of the Wallace Serving Bar Cart as the anti-bar cart. The wooden design resembles a sleek-looking side table thanks to its grounded base, but underneath, a discreet set of wheels gives you the ability to roll the decor piece anywhere a gin and tonic or negroni needs mixing. Its clandestine silhouette also allows it to double as a shelf for non-drink making goods like magazines, coffee table books, or your favorite potted plants.

While this circular cart might lack ample space for large-scale barware, its Art Deco style gives it an Old Hollywood feel. Placed in an area that gets lots of sunshine, the amber tempered glass shelves diffuse a warm glow, layering in some unexpected coziness during cloudless days.

Business & Pleasure Co.’s Al Fresco bar cart is a throwback to a time of leisure under the Parisian sun. While the cart can be used indoors, we can’t imagine a better place for it than on a terrace surrounded by a garden. As if the charming scalloped trim wasn’t enough, the designated umbrella hole nails the summery vibe for poolside mojitos or curbside lemonade stands.

To find that heirloom quality of Victorian design lore, look no further than Greenrow’s Liona bar cart with Barley twist legs to charm you an dyour guests. The two shelves in a chestnut finish offer ample space for glassware and a floral arrangement, should you want to create a whole spread for summer spritzers.

Here’s a piece of decor with lots of Mad Men energy. With its sloped legs and clean-lined silhouette, this cart has enough midcentury-modern charm to appeal to retro design lovers. Two full-size shelves have ample room for old fashioned and martini ingredients, while a mid-tier half shelf can either support a few extra bottles or a stack of your favorite mixology books.

For studio apartment dwellers or homes where square footage is precious, this miniature cart is slim enough to slip into discreet spaces until happy hour rolls around. Made of sturdy beech wood, rounded edges and a light hue soften the beverage cart’s overall look. We think these elements also make it well-suited for adorning Scandi- or Japandi-centric spaces.

Leather accents, exposed wood grain shelves, and an antiqued gold finish bring a polished look and feel to this spacious bar accessory. Two levels with a generous amount of clearance makes it easy to style, whether you’re packing it full of bottles or prefer a more curated decor approach of sculptural glassware.

This sleek, minimalist bar cart will stay with you for many moves. The chrome steel frame defines the iconic silhouette, and you can choose from nine powder-coated steel panels that come in classic black and white, as well as ultra-bold yellow and emerald.

For Bauhaus style on a budget, the Cullin bar cart is more than ready to serve. The angular silhouette resembles less of a cart and more of a full on booze-fueled vehicle, and feels especially cheerful in green or brick red colorways (white and black are also available). With storage for four wine bottles, you can stock multiple kinds of sippers, from sparkling to organic, to please even the snobbiest of guests.

Sturdy and sustainably sourced, this midcentury beauty from West Elm is made in a Fair Trade facility from FSC-certified kiln-dried eucalyptus wood. The soft honey finish of acacia veneer gives this piece accessibility, while the antique brass details add a touch of flair.
