#4941. How a Society Photographer Fell in Love With an Unexpected Upstate Victorian Home

I had no intention of buying the house on Willard Place. I only added it to the short list of Hudson properties I planned to visit out of sheer morbid curiosity. The house looked completely crazy online, with its wild colors and eccentric embellishments, inside and out—a confounding mix of hideous and fabulous. I felt compelled to check it out in person. Real estate agent extraordinaire Julia Crowley made the appointment at Willard the first stop on our house-hunting itinerary. The artist Helen Marden, a dear friend and owner of the nearby Hotel Tivoli, decided to come along for the ride. “What are we doing here?” she asked when we got out of the car. I’d forgotten to mention this purely voyeuristic side trip.

The polychrome exterior of Craig-Martin's house in Hudson, New York.

Craig-Martin on the front steps of her home with her beloved Roo, a whippet and chihuahua mix.

Above the mantel hangs a painting by Haitian artist Ramphis Magloire. To the left is an etching by Brice Marden, a gift from the artist to Craig-Martin’s son on the occasion of his birth. To the right is a Fernand Léger print, a gift from the homeowner’s artist father, Michael Craig-Martin. A mix of Indian, Moroccan, Italian, Chinese, and Indonesian art and furnishings testifies to a well-traveled life.

I was fully expecting the polychrome Victorian house to be crammed full of furniture, as seen in the online listing. But when Helen, Julia, and I stepped in, it was breathtakingly empty, save for shafts of winter sunlight that illuminated the generous rooms and old wood floors. It became clear that the grotesque furniture I’d seen in the photos was virtual—a terrifying misuse of AI. We sat on the old iron radiators, quietly absorbing the beautiful proportions and unexpected calm. It was fabulous—that was undeniable—but with five bedrooms, it was far more house than I was looking for, and seemed daunting for one person. Helen disagreed. At the next stop, she took one look at the house from the car and didn’t bother to get out.

Craig-Martin seated on a chaise on the terrace off the dining room. A painting by Joe Brainard hangs above a vase from Regan & Smith in Hudson, New York.

George Nakashima chairs surround a table from India made of dismantled boat parts.

An Indonesian stool sits beside a rattan chaise beneath an Indian elephant mobile on the terrace.

A barracuda sculpture by Dalwa Donoffre rests on an Aalto stool in the window bay of the dining room. Beside it, an Ashley Bickerton sculpture sits on a marble pedestal from Rajasthan, surrounded by Stone Age tools, a whale vertebra, and a turquoise bowl by Mary Heilmann.

In the entry hall, a Josef Albers print is installed above a rustic table with a Cameroonian fertility figure.

Over the following months I continued looking in the area, but nothing struck a chord. “You should be thinking about Willard,” Helen kept saying. “It’s a good house.” Completed in 1875, the classic Victorian was built during the whaling boom that enriched Hudson and dotted the town with other equally eccentric Victorian homes replete with multiple bow windows, terraces, and turrets. It had been standing empty and neglected for years, but a thorough assessment did not threaten any looming “old house” disasters. Unable to get the beauty of the place out of my mind, I made an offer.

An ant sculpture from Deyrolle in Paris joins an array of copper pans in the kitchen.

A pastel drawing by Finbar Craig-Martin hangs above a Chinese console table with bagel sculptures by Hanna Liden.

I enlisted a friend to help me move in, though furniture would have to come later. We drove up from the city in a rented van filled with Moroccan rugs and two inflatable mattresses, and ate pizza on the floor in front of a roaring fire. Two years later, I can’t imagine living without the house’s spaciousness and light, or its grand old-world flourishes. Frankly, I don’t know how I coped for so many years without a Norma Desmond staircase. It seems unrealistic now to imagine life without one, which confirms my suspicion that luxury is a one-way trip.

Artworks in the primary bedroom include (clockwise from far left) a painting by Michael Craig-Martin, a painting by Charlie Lucas (above mantel), a light sculpture by Theo Rosenblum (on mantel), a Jessica Craig-Martin photograph of Fran Lebowitz (floor), and a round painting on glass by Finbar Craig-Martin.

A crayon drawing of the homeowner at age two by her mother, Jan Hashey, sits on a Balinese child’s chair in the bathroom.

Jessica Craig-Martin in the bedroom of her son, Finbar. To the right of a movie poster from Ghana is a photograph by Jason Noyes.

Craig-Martin’s son’s room has a Paul McCobb desk, an Eames chair modified by artists Eric Wesley and Piero Golia. and a poster of Ursula Andress from the movie Dr. No above a rustic kitchen cabinet.

In Finbar’s room, a Nate Lowman pizza painting hangs beside a foam-core cutout of David Bowie.

I added a red refrigerator and stove to match the kitchen’s vintage red Formica countertops, and a lifetime’s worth of art and ephemera, from artwork by my parents and friends to artifacts, textiles, and natural oddities collected on various travels. There are unnatural oddities, too. For example, I have a weakness for cheap Japanese plushy cushions, my favorite being a huge pink peach that, when viewed from a certain angle, morphs unmistakably into a huge pink ass. It is also evident that I favor crocodiles and alligators in any form, though if you asked me why, I couldn’t say. One thing is certain: We are a very long way from “form follows function.” Fortunately, at least to my eye, the house absorbed my proclivity for both the ridiculous and the sublime with grace. Helen was right. It is a good house, and it has welcomed me, perhaps recognizing in me a madness that in some way matches its own.

The home office/library features an Alvar Aalto chair and stool, a low alligator table Craig-Martin found on the street in New York City, and a totem from the Solomon Islands displayed beside the fireplace. On the mantel, a Michael Craig-Martin hammer sculpture is surmounted by a Brice Marden etching. A tower of Hermès boxes holds a corner of the room.

Detail of the Hermès box tower. Behind it is an etching by Joel Shapiro, a gift from the artist.

Exterior view of Willard House and its eccentric, polychromatic Victorian façade.

#4939. Meet the Athlete-Turned-Founder Rethinking the Sports Bra

Sara Falkson turned her own experience with ill-fitting sports bras into a brand focused on better support.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

When an athlete suits up for competition, every piece of equipment matters. A shin guard that doesn’t fit or football pads that aren’t secured properly can affect performance. But one essential piece of gear is often overlooked: the sports bra.

When shopping at top athletic retailers, female athletes are usually presented with around three options: small, medium, and large. The cup size grows with the band size, and there is little to no room for customization.

Anyone with breasts can relate to struggling to find a comfortable bra, but it's an unfortunate reality many female athletes have come to accept. What may seem like a trivial aspect of an athlete’s uniform holds a much greater significance. According to a 2021 survey of 70 elite athletes conducted by the English Institute of Sport, 75 percent of athletes have never been fitted for a sports bra, and 26 percent reported that breast pain affects their athletic performance. This speaks to the bigger picture that 46 percent of school-aged women said their breasts were a barrier to exercise.

Trying to find the solution for an ill-fitting sports bra often falls on the consumer—but Robyn founder Sara Falkson wants to change that.

Falkson grew up in a bustling home filled with love for sports and love for one another. In the middle with three older brothers and one younger sister, she tried almost every sport, but eventually settled on field hockey.

Playing sports while going through puberty is something all adolescents have to deal with, and Falkson was no exception. Navigating a changing body and mind can be tricky, especially when you’re relying on that body to remember plays and execute drills.

When it came time for the obligatory mall trip to shop for sports bras, Falkson says she found herself relying on her teammates.

“My teammates were really the best sisters of all,” she tells Women’s Health. “I found such a family and community in sports.”

Her teammates offered emotional support, but Falkson still struggled to find physical support.

“I have a bigger chest, and when it came to finding a sports bra, I struggled so deeply,” she says. “I would either find some that would be over-sexualizing, or I would chafe, and I would get horrible scars and stabs.”

A solution she and her mom came up with was for her to wear two sports bras while playing field hockey. Although this remedy did solve the support issue, Falkson says it came with another set of issues—teammates commented on it, and it wasn’t comfortable.

“Early on, I just assumed that my body’s probably the problem,” she says. “This gear design came from huge companies. They know what they’re doing, and I’m probably the one not fitting the mold.”

This prompted her to look toward other solutions, such as modifying her fueling regime to change her body, as well as the more permanent solution of a breast reduction.

These experiences stayed with Falkson, including through her time on the Dartmouth College field hockey team. She says that even in college, a lot of her teammates were still navigating the same issues of pressure and appearance on the field.

“We tried to do as much as possible to bring in the resources on the mental performance side and the nutrition side at Dartmouth and really switch the language [to] being more performance-focused, rather than appearance,” she says.

After earning her undergraduate degree, Falkson moved to San Francisco and began coaching youth field hockey on the side. She loved coaching, but soon heard familiar concerns from her players.

“The girls were saying the exact same things,” she says. “They were focused more on how they looked in their uniforms than how they were actually performing, and by that point, I’m like, ‘This is ridiculous. Why do so many of us feel this way?’”

Motivated by her frustration, Falkson enrolled at Harvard University to pursue a master’s degree in design and engineering. While there, a single stat stayed on her mind: 50 percent of girls drop out of sport by age 14 due to low body confidence, according to research conducted by Dove.

“It was so heartbreaking, but also in some ways validating knowing that there’s some research grounded in this, and we’re not alone,” she says.

While at Harvard, Falkson started leading body confidence workshops in the Boston area where she sat down with young female athletes to get a better understanding on what could be done to help them feel better about playing sports.

“We put the ownership in their hands, and that’s how we got Robyn,” she says of her company, an athletic brand dedicated to designing sports bras that elevate body confidence on and off the field. “Time after time, girls were so frustrated about sports bras, because they’re an essential piece of equipment, but so overlooked.”

Robyn started as a master’s thesis proposal. Falkson’s advisor, Karen Korellis Reuther, played a key role.

Korellis Reuther is extremely familiar with the world of design. Now a Design Critic in Architecture in Harvard’s Graduate School of Design, she is a designer by training and practice for over 40 years and is a former creative executive at Nike and Reebok.

Korellis Reuther’s passion for helping to redesign the world for women also was fueled by personal experience.

“As designers, we really connect very much to the built world, and that’s where Sara really had a passion,” Korellis Reuther says, “We were trying to understand what around the built world—whether it’s what we put on our bodies, what we use, or what we surround ourselves with—that is causing girls’ body confidence to drop, and what could we do from a design perspective?”

Korellis Reuther’s research in women-focused design goes far beyond sports. She is the author of Man-Made: How We Designed a World That Leaves Women Out, and How We Can Make It Right.

In the book, Korellis Reuther explores how women are still grossly unaccounted for in the world of design, despite inhabiting the same world as men. Her research examines how components of everyday life inconvenience women—and sometimes endanger them.

From poor locker room setups to unfit fire equipment gear for female firefighters and personal protection equipment, women are consistently thought of as an afterthought when it comes to design.

“We think, ‘I can’t make this work,’” she says. “But the truth is, it wasn’t designed for us in the first place.”

While working as a design executive for major brands, Korellis Reuther had a unique perspective being physically present where these decisions were made, but still not feeling like change was taking place. She speaks openly about feeling dismissed and looked over in important meetings, despite her high-ranking position in the company.

“The story had to be told, and as a designer, I knew we could make it right.”

Robyn is set to release a high-support bra, the Game Day, and a medium-support bra, the Everyday. The principal component that makes Robyn bras different from other athletic brands is the adjustability features. Almost all components of the bra will be adjustable, including the shoulder straps and bottom band.

In preliminary testing, subjects ran at 8 MPH pace while hooked up to machines to monitor their breast movement. The Robyn Bra demonstrated approximately 46 percent less vertical breast movement than a leading high-support sports bra.

Robyn encompasses Falkson, including the brand’s name. She and her mother share Robyn as a middle name. It proved to have a double meaning after Korellis Reuther pointed out that the robin is one of the only species of bird with a distinct breast that is a different color then the rest of the body.

“Just like the robin bird, we want our athlete to lead with color, joy, and strength,” Falkson says.

Robyn has certainly been led with strength thus far. With the product set to launch this spring, Robyn has already amassed over 34,000 followers on Instagram as well as more than 100 brand ambassadors comprised of high school and college athletes.

It has already been a part of five major collaborations, and was featured in Yankees Mag—another element that’s personal to Falkson, as her longtime boyfriend, Ben Rice, plays professional baseball for the New York Yankees.

The couple went to high school together and began dating early in college. Rice will make the occasional surprise appearance on Robyn’s social media, like when Falkson handmade a coffee table to celebrate his anniversary with the team. She also filmed a series in which she 3D printed custom Yankees memorabilia for this year’s playoff season.

Both athletes, Falkson says the two are able to push each other to be better, and seeing Rice’s career expand has made her more inspired about her own.

“I think if anything, seeing his career has just inspired me to also push to see my dreams and take risks,” she says.

Robyn isn’t just about apparel—it has its own mission, too. The company has hosted dozens of body confidence workshops, including a recent partnership with South Bronx United, a soccer nonprofit.. Robyn teamed up with South Bronx United as well as mental health organization The Hidden Opponent to create a four week-curriculum focused on empowering young athletes.

Robyn also works with Shooting Touch, a basketball nonprofit out of Boston for body confidence-focused programming. Falkson also coordinated a partnership with a 3D printing company to bring the printers into local schools to encourage more girls to get into STEM.

“For me personally, I’m glad our world has Saras, because we need to keep pushing forward,” Korellis Reuther says. “She pushes forward from her true, authentic self—with a sense of joy, with a sense of optimism, but also a sense of, ‘We haven’t done it right yet, and we need to make this right.’”

Looking toward the future, Falkson says her dream scenario would be to have Robyn gear become part of a female athlete’s uniform. “Once I see Robyn in the real world and girls connecting and bonding over a body confident sport and feeling their best, celebrating lifelong movement, that’s my goal.”

Through it all, Falkson has held on to the perspective she had as a young athlete.

“Growing up and being a woman is hard,” she says. “What you’re going through is okay and normal, and you’re still going to be valued. Giving girls the resources to have that space is really important.”

#4936. Your ‘Healthy’ Soda Alternative May Not Be So Healthy for Your Liver

WE'RE LONG PAST having to make a decision between just Coke or Pepsi. You've got a lot of options if you're bored of water—from fizzy nonalcoholic drinks to flavored seltzers. But while a lot of these drinks market themselves as healthy alternatives to sodas, research suggests they could be silently doing a number on your liver health.

If you've done the work to switch to diet sodas or have learned to love prebiotic sodas like Olipop or Poppi, the news can be pretty hard to hear. So it's important to get this out of the way upfront: If you have a "healthy" soda every once in a while, the experts we spoke to say the risk of having liver problems is unlikely. The real danger to your liver is if you’re tossing these back on a daily or near-daily basis. Here's everything you need to know.

THE STUDY PRESENTED at the United European Gastroenterology (UEG) Week 2025 used data from 103,251 people who enrolled in the UK Biobank and didn’t have liver disease at the start of the study. Participants were asked to fill out questionnaires about what they ate and drank throughout the day. The researchers then paid close attention to how much sugar-sweetened and low- or non-sugar-sweetened beverages they drank.

The major takeaway from the study is that it pretty much linked any drink that wasn’t water to metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD). You might know it by its former name, nonalcoholic fatty liver disease (NAFLD). But whatever you call it, it's no joke.

MASLD happens when too much fat builds up in your liver from inputs other than alcohol. It can lead to serious liver scarring or end-stage liver disease. It can also put you at high risk for heart disease and diabetes. According to the American College of Gastroenterology, heart disease is the number one killer of people living with MASLD.

DAILY DRINKING OF nearly 9 ounces of any type of drink that was not water was associated with a significantly higher risk of developing MASLD. That risk jumped 50 percent for sugar-sweetened beverages and was surprisingly higher—60 percent—for those who drank low- or non-sugar-sweetened beverages. (Though some of these prebiotic sodas have more sugar and less fiber than marketing claims might imply).

After a 10-year follow-up, 949 people developed MASLD and 103 died from liver-related causes. Both types of beverages were significantly connected to increases in liver fat.

Replacing nearly 12 ounces of sugary beverages with water lowered MASLD risk by 14.4 percent and by 13.5 percent with low- or non-sugar-sweetened drinks. But if you switched completely from a sugary beverage like soda to a low-sugar beverage like fruit juice, the authors found that this did not help lower a person's risk of developing MASLD.

Exactly which beverages raised risk is hard to say because the authors did not directly state what low- or no-sugar drinks people were drinking. That said, there are lots of sodas that are marketed as being healthier alternatives that are not as good as they claim, according to Danbee Kim, MD, nutrition expert and assistant professor at Rutgers New Jersey Medical School.

“Even beverages marketed as ‘natural’ or ‘health-oriented’ may still fall into this category if they rely on sweeteners instead of sugar,” Kim says. “The key commonality is that they provide sweetness without significant calories. However, they may still affect liver health.”

A FEW THINGS may be going on here. “Low/non-sugar sweetened beverages, although low in calories, may still affect liver health through several pathways,” says Lihe Liu, a graduate student researcher at The First Affiliated Hospital of Soochow University in China and lead study author. “They can alter the gut microbiome, interfere with feelings of fullness, increase preference for sweet foods, and in some cases stimulate insulin secretion.”

When you have something that’s sweet, even if it doesn’t contain much or any sugar, it sets off a chain reaction in your body the same way as if you were to actually have sugar, says Albert Matheny, RD, CSCS, registered dietitian and co-founder of SoHo Strength Lab. “You’re basically tricking your body,” he says. “Your body still reacts as if it has sugar.”

As a result, Matheny says your body produces more insulin (a hormone that helps regulate blood sugar), and encourages the liver to take in more sugar from your bloodstream. This can lead to metabolic disruption.

“Some artificial sweeteners may alter how the body handles glucose and insulin, contributing to insulin resistance, which is a key driver of metabolic dysfunction and fat accumulation in the liver,” says Kim. This process may even mess with your appetite, making you think that you're hungry when you're not.

Another reason has nothing to do with your gut, but with your mind. Kim says we can't discount the possibility that people who drink these low- and non-sugar-sweetened beverages may subconsciously compensate for the “saved” calories by eating more sugary or fatty foods later.

Keep in mind that all this is speculation. The explanation for why and how artificially sweetened beverages are linked to MASLD is still under investigation since Liu says her team didn't look too deep into this.

NOT NECESSARILY. “THEY’RE definitely an improvement over traditional diet sodas,” Matheny says. “That’s not to say that they’re a totally healthy option.”

When given a choice, water reigns supreme. And even then you still don't want to go too overboard.

If you still want to crack open a cranberry fizz prebiotic soda or even an unsweetened iced tea, Matheny says that’s probably fine, too. “In moderation, kind of everything is OK.”

Korin Miller is a seasoned freelance writer specializing in general wellness, sexual health and relationships, and lifestyle trends, with work appearing in Men’s Health, Women’s Health, Everyday Health, Food & Wine, and more. She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

#4928. The 17 Most Beautiful Ski Resorts to Visit This Winter—Including Three in the US

When winter arrives, the world’s most idyllic mountain towns transform into playgrounds for serious adrenaline seekers. But, as the most beautiful ski resorts prove, the appeal of an extraordinary alpine escape extends well beyond its slopes. Why kick off your ski boots just anywhere when you could do so in a castle-like hotel in the Swiss Alps or a secluded Japanese ryokan tucked among snow-covered forests? Sure, the world-renowned runs are worth traveling for, but sometimes so are the ski hotels themselves. Think Michelin-starred restaurants, award-winning spas, art-filled interiors, and architecture that makes the most of its dramatic surroundings. Here, AD rounds up 17 of the most beautiful ski resorts to visit this winter, whether you’re an alpine ace, a beginner ski bum, or purely an après enthusiast.

Getting to Zaborin Ryokan is an adventure in itself, which means you know it’ll be good. After landing at New Chitose Airport in Sapporo, it’s a two-hour drive to the ryokan, which is hidden in the Hanazono woods of Hokkaido. Arriving at the property feels like discovering some sort of secretive lair from a Bond flick—a series of low-lying villas covered in snow suddenly reveal themselves. Built on natural hot springs, each of the villas has its own indoor and outdoor onsen, plus floor-to-ceiling windows and a private balcony with views of Mount Yōtei, the area’s dormant volcano, in the distance. Make an appointment with the in-house matcha master and prepare your taste buds for a multicourse Japanese kaiseki meal. If you can tear yourself away from your absurdly serene suite long enough to ski or snowboard, the ryokan will arrange your transfer to and from Hanazono Niseko ski area, just seven minutes away by car.

Modernist design meets mountainside adventure at Pendry Park City, a ski-in, ski-out luxury resort where “hitting the slopes” means basically just stepping outside. Tucked in Canyons Village at Park City Mountain—the largest ski resort in the US, with over 7,000 skiable acres spread across two connected mountains—this slopeside sanctuary is right next to the brand-new, high-speed Sunrise Gondola. After a day appreciating the powder, guests gather at Après Pendry for fondue and crème brulée espresso martinis either by the fire or outside. Alternatively, they head up to the rooftop for a dip in the year-round heated pool and hot tub, plus immaculate mountain views.

Located in St. Moritz, Switzerland, the Carlton Hotel boasts 60 luxurious suites that blend old-world grandeur with contemporary elements. The resort is also home to the Michelin-starred restaurant Da Vittorio.

The Gstaad Palace in Switzerland opened in 1913 and has catered to jet-setters and stars such as Elizabeth Taylor, Grace Kelly, and Marlene Dietrich throughout its long history. The castlelike hotel overlooks the streets of Gstaad, which are filled with high-end boutiques.

The Pines Lodge, a RockResort is a rustic Colorado retreat with ski-in, ski-out access set above Beaver Creek Village. After a long day on the slopes, guests can enjoy views of the Rockies from the acclaimed Grouse Mountain Grill.

Architect Mario Botta designed the striking spa at the Tschuggen Grand Hotel in Arosa, Switzerland. The alpine retreat, which was built in 1929, features 130 rooms and suites with opulent interiors by Carlo Rampazzi.

Designed by architect Sybille de Margerie, the intimate Cheval Blanc Courchevel in Courchevel, France, is the ultimate in alpine luxury. The resort, which is owned by LVMH chairman Bernard Arnault, is also filled with blue-chip artwork by Michel Othoniel, Xavier Veillant, and Bruno Peinado, whose Trojan Horse sculpture is seen here.

Founded by railway tycoon William Cornelius Van Horne in 1888, the Fairmont Banff Springs is set within Banff National Park and offers stunning views of the Canadian Rockies. The sprawling hotel, which was inspired by a Scottish baronial castle, includes 764 rooms and suites and nine restaurants and bars.

The St. Regis Aspen Resort in the Colorado Rockies received a $40 million makeover by interior architect Lauren Rottet in 2012. Set at the base of Aspen Mountain, the hotel includes 179 rooms and suites outfitted with Ralph Lauren beds and desks and marble-lined baths.

The ski-in, ski-out Hôtel des Dromonts in Avoriaz, France, was built in the 1960s by architect Jacques Labro, who was inspired by the work of Le Corbusier and Charlotte Perriand. The interiors of the distinctive angular building were recently refreshed and upgraded by designer Jocelyne Sibuet.

Munich-based firm Oikios Architect took inspiration from pine cones for the design of the InterContinental Davos in Davos, Switzerland. The oval façade uses over 790 curved, overlapping steel panels to create the scaled appearance. Inside, 216 tasteful guest rooms overlook the city—home to the World Economic Forum—and surrounding mountains.

Offering a unique combination of Asian-inspired design motifs and alpine rustic touches, The Chedi Andermatt is one of Switzerland’s most wonderful offerings. The boutique hotel is located 4,747 feet above sea level and includes a 25,833-square-foot spa to take advantage of when you’re not out on the slopes.

With rustic and understated architecture and interiors, Le Chalet Zanier is designed to mimic the natural beauty of the surrounding Mont Blanc mountain range. The resort is made up of a 12-room main structure as well as a separate three-bedroom private cottage. Like the snowflakes that top the roofs, no room is exactly like any of the others.

Located in a densely wooded area, Forestis is situated exactly where the Austrian monarchy established a tuberculosis sanatorium in 1912. Designed with simplicity in mind, the rich stone, wood, and glass of the hotel are inspired by the nature that surrounds it.

#4925. How to Find Out If You Got the Measles Vaccine as a Kid

The US may lose its measles elimination status after multiple outbreaks in the past two years.

THE US is likely to lose its measles elimination status this week when the Pan-American Health Organization, part of the public health arm of the United Nations, meets to review data from the multiple outbreaks that have occurred nationwide in the past two years.As of early November, there are 5,182 confirmed measles cases in 47 states. This is more than double of cases in 2025—the CDC reported 2,289 measles cases in 45 states. And a far cry from when measles were practically eliminated in the US with a record low of 86 cases two decades ago.

Of all the US cases this year, 96 percent come from people who are unvaccinated. Since most of us got the MMR (measles, mumps, and rubella) vaccine as a kid, it wouldn't hurt to double-check that you're protected against one of the most contagious infectious diseases in the world.

Now, there's no central repository of childhood vaccine records in the United States, says Brian Labus, PhD, MPH, assistant professor of epidemiology and biostatistics at the University of Nevada, Las Vegas. So you'd need to find your copy of your vaccination records or get them from your pediatrician's office—which may or may not still be operating. There are a few other places to do that detective work (more below).

In lieu of that, some people are getting blood tests to check if they have antibodies for some of the diseases children are vaccinated for, but there are pros and cons with those. Here's what to know about making sure you're protected against measles and whether you got other vaccinations on the childhood immunization schedule.

IF YOU WERE born before 1957, it’s assumed that you were exposed to measles as a child and don’t need to be vaccinated, Labus says. If you’re around 45 or older, you were likely exposed to chicken pox and don’t need that vaccine, adds Thomas Russo, MD, chief of the division of infectious diseases at the University at Buffalo. There aren’t specific age cutoffs for other diseases, though.

But there is a blood test, called an antibody serology test, that can tell you if you have antibodies against certain diseases, including measles, mumps, chicken pox, hepatitis, tetanus, HIV, mononucleosis, and others.

These blood tests can suggest whether you’ve been previously infected with these diseases or vaccinated against them, Dr. Russo says. “The presence of antibodies indicates that your body mounted an immune response against a pathogen either due to a natural exposure or to a vaccination,” Labus explains. “It can also give you an idea about the level of protection you have in your body.”

While that generally means that you’re immune, it’s also not a guarantee, says Georges Benjamin, MD, executive director of the American Public Health Association. Also, antibodies decline over time.

Starting with an antibody test to determine your vaccination status might make sense for some people, but Labus says there are some practicalities to consider.

For one, the tests can be expensive, costing around $250, Dr. Benjamin says. Your insurance may or may not cover them.

It can also take several days to get the results. Labus says if you’re using the test to determine if you need a vaccine quickly, you’re potentially not protected during that wait. “While this doesn't matter for routine situations, it could be the difference between getting measles or avoiding it if you live in a community experiencing an outbreak,” he says.

If you're not sure you were vaccinated, Labus and the other doctors we spoke to suggest just getting the MMR, polio, DTap (diphtheria, tetanus, and acellular pertussis), and other shots just to be safe (more about that below).

But if you're not sure you want to get jabbed, your pediatrician retired long ago, and you have a moment to invest in detective work on your records, here's where to look:

• Old records you or your parents have at home, like a baby book.

• Any schools you attended. Many states require certain vaccines before you start kindergarten, including MMR, polio, DTap, and varicella, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. If you were in the military, you would have been required to get certain vaccines.

• The health department where you grew up. Some state health departments keep immunization records, Dr. Benjamin says. But whether yours are there may depend on your age. “State immunization registries are a relatively new development, and those systems were not likely in place when you were young if you are more than 25 or 30 years old,” Labus says.

TALK TO YOUR doctor about your concerns and uncertainty about your childhood vaccination status and level of protection against diseases, and they can suggest whether just getting the vaccine for the first time or a booster is appropriate, Labus says. “Most vaccines can be given to adults without any problems.”

Getting the shot is usually cheaper and faster than an antibody test, he adds. Dr. Benjamin points out that boosters are a good idea to consider especially if you have chronic diseases, immune system dysfunction, or work in the healthcare industry. Also consider it if you plan to travel to parts of the world with higher instances of diseases like measles, such as countries in the Middle East, Dr. Russo adds.

And by the way, even if you did get all your vaccines as a child, you still need to get a DTap or Tdap booster every 10 years as an adult for protection against diphtheria and tetanus, Dr. Russo says.

For the other childhood vaccines, “receiving an extra dose is going to boost your antibody levels even if you have previously been vaccinated, which is a good thing,” Labus explains. Dr. Russo agrees: “The path of least resistance is when in doubt, there’s no harm in getting a booster shot.”

#4923. Etsy Says Gah! The Handmade Marketplace and Playful Fashion Label Team Up on a Home Collection

Since its founding in 2015, Lisa Says Gah has become known for its bowtie-pasta cardigans, checkerboard coats, and swans. Today, the California-based label is releasing its first home collection, which has a similarly playful ethos: “The Lisa Says Gah world really naturally extends into the home,” says founder Lisa Bühler. “Home and clothing ultimately tell the story of who you are.”

A pin board and two eclectic frames from Frame Mavens comprise a third of the collection.

Founder Lisa Bühler co-judged the Etsy Design Awards and selected three winners to co-design Etsy Says Gah!, her brand’s first foray into pieces for the home.

The collection is a collaboration with Etsy, a platform Bühler often uses for her own inspiration (things that make her shout: Gah!). “When you go to Etsy, you’re always going to find something that delights you,” she says.

Lisa Says Gah has stocked independent designers alongside its own line for more than a decade. This year, Bühler co-judged the Etsy Design Awards, and selected three winners to co-design Etsy Says Gah!, a nine-piece collection exclusively available via Etsy. Bühler says she prioritized “unexpected, collected things that you just don’t automatically go to when you think of homeware.”

UK-based Cait Mellor executed Bühler’s mood board through crochet in a floral table runner, a horseshoe decal, and swan coasters.

The home collection’s foundational inspiration comes from Lisa Says Gah’s two fall lines, Pastoral Daze and Swan Dance, which pair horses and swans with paisleys and cabin-inspired knits. Bühler worked with the Etsy designers by sharing mood boards, giving notes when she wanted more of a motif or a different color combination, and trusting each maker with her own medium. “They’re the expert[s],” she says. “The process felt like really trusting and putting it in their hands.”’

Each maker applied the same mood board differently. Jacqueline Sanchez, a Houston-based ceramicist who won this year’s Grand Prize for a vase covered in more than 1,500 hand-applied scales, sculpted the swan in relief on a pale pink vase wound with green vines. She also set a horse’s head in a blue baroque cameo on a cream mug and dotted a wavy platter with tiny fruit as a nod to the brand’s food prints. “[I liked] having the freedom to incorporate elements that feel really true to my work,” says Sanchez. The vase is a piece Bühler keeps coming back to: “You would just be happy to have it on the table without flowers,” she says.

The vase is the piece Bühler keeps coming back to: “You would just be happy to have it on the table without flowers,” she says.

“Because it’s our first home collection, we wanted it to feel like something you collect over time in your house versus a uniform grouping,” says Bühler.

Cait Mellor, a UK-based seller, worked the swan and horse elements into the open grid of filet crochet: swans on a set of coasters, a horseshoe under the word Lucky on a small tapestry, and flowers down a cream table runner. Her goal is to “bring this traditional art form into a more contemporary setting.” Bühler is already picturing the coasters at dinner parties, where “everyone is going to talk about the swans.”

Emma Miller and Kristen Kellar, chosen for their fabric-wrapped frames, focused on color and pattern rather than the animals. They made a burgundy frame with black polka dots on a blue background, a frame featuring blue pixelated flowers that look almost cross-stitched, and a pin board that uses pink fabric crossed with orange ribbon.

The home collection’s foundational inspiration comes from Lisa Says Gah’s two fall lines, Pastoral Daze and Swan Dance, which pair horses and swans with paisleys and cabin-inspired knits.

Bühler didn’t want the nine pieces to look like a matched set. “Because it’s our first home collection, we wanted it to feel like something you collect over time in your house versus a uniform grouping,” she says. Each item costs less than $200, and with the holidays coming—with endless-feeling opportunities for hosting and gifting—now is a great time to start collecting.

#4922. This Is How Long Body Recomposition Takes, According to a Physical Therapist

Plus, why it's the fitness goal I recommend to everyone.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

As a fitness professional and doctor of physical therapy, I’ve always been active. I spent most of my 20s chasing the “toned” look (which, for the record, isn’t even a real thing; it’s marketing jargon). I did all the classic workouts that promised to get me there—high reps, low weights, endless cardio—and prided myself on leaving every class drenched in sweat. I ate as little as possible because I thought that would give me the body I wanted.

Like so many women, I was lean from undereating and overexercising, but I never felt strong—and I definitely didn’t feel good. I had chronic pain, fatigue, and lived on a hamster wheel of more workouts and less food.

Everything changed when I started focusing on building muscle and body recomposition instead. It turns out, what most people mean by “getting toned” is actually body recomp, which, put simply, is losing fat and gaining muscle at the same time.

There’s just one problem. How you get to body recomposition is entirely different from what we’ve been told, especially as women. It’s the ultimate anti–yo-yo goal—and it’s about consistency, not extremes.

I learned how to train for hypertrophy, eat to support recovery, and actually rest—and I got the look (and feel!) I was searching for faster than I expected. Within a year of changing my approach to fitness, I gained eight pounds of muscle and lost roughly five pounds of fat with fewer workouts, more food, no joint pain, and way less effort.

If you’re about to start this journey, here’s how long you can expect it to take.

I’ve successfully recomposed twice and plan to do it again soon, as I’ve just had my second baby.

The first time, I gained eight pounds of muscle and lost five pounds of fat over a year. The second time, postpartum, I gained 4.2 pounds of muscle and lost fat in just a few months.

Muscle tends to come back faster the second time because when you’ve built it once, your muscle fibers retain extra myonuclei (essentially the “brains” of the muscle), which remain even if you lose muscle mass. When you train again, those nuclei help muscle growth happen faster.

That’s why building muscle now is such a valuable investment for your future self.

Body recomposition is a slower process, but that’s exactly why I like it. It forces you to slow down and implement sustainable changes with your training, your nutrition, and your recovery.

It’s tempting to get to your ideal body composition as fast as possible, but rushing it might only make things take longer in the end. For example, overtraining can lead to injury, sidelining you from training, and undereating can prevent muscle growth—or worse, lead to losing it. While this article focuses on the strength training that goes into body recomposition, nutrition and fueling properly are essential because when you’re in too big of a calorie deficit, you can lose both muscle and fat.

Month 1:You might see early fat loss in the first month if you’re in a calorie deficit. Muscle growth typically takes longer, but beginner exercisers might see small developments more quickly than longer-trained individuals.

Month 2:You’ll start to feel stronger as your nervous system adapts and you gain better coordination. You might see muscle definition toward the end of the month because growth can take anywhere from eight to 12 weeks.

Month 3:You’ll likely begin to see visible muscle definition and continued fat loss.

Month 4+:Progress continues, though it’s normal to have slower or plateau phases. Early in training, neuromuscular strength and protein synthesis are more rapid, but as your body becomes accustomed to the stimulus, the improvements can slow. In this phase, it’s important to refine your habits, train close to failure, and find what keeps you consistent.

You can’t rush the process. Building muscle and losing fat simultaneously requires balance—not extremes. If you’re in a 200-to-400-calorie deficit, expect around 0.5 to 1 pound of fat loss per week and visible muscle growth within 8 to 12 weeks, having made good progress toward your goal in three months.

Of course, body recomposition is only possible if you’re focusing on the right things. These are the four most important factors.

Nutrition: Supports Fat Loss And Muscle Growth

I’m not formally trained in nutrition, but here’s what I’ve learned from registered dietitians and what works for me and the Evlo community:

Tracking your food, especially in the beginning, can be incredibly eye-opening. I found I was eating more calories than I realized—and not nearly enough protein. Tracking isn’t for everyone, but it’s a useful tool to build awareness. You can use apps like MyFitnessPal or even list all your food into ChatGPT.

Get started with our Eating for Body Recomp guide

There’s no such thing as a “toning” workout. Fat loss comes primarily from nutrition, not exercise—and you can’t spot-reduce fat. Exercise builds muscle, but only if it’s done with enough mechanical tension and intensity.

Remember: the “burn” you feel during high-rep workouts isn’t what builds muscle. Muscle growth happens from mechanical tension, not just fatigue. That’s why classic “toning” workouts rarely deliver the promise; they’re too light to drive hypertrophy.

Cardio and Activity: Supports Energy Expenditure

Cardio and daily movement are still important, but probably not for the reasons you think.

Although debated in the literature, calories burned from exercise may not be fully additive, especially when in a calorie deficit for recomp. Doing more cardio doesn’t necessarily mean you’ll burn more fat. And, because we want to keep you fresh and not overworked for your strength workouts, I’d say don't overdo it on the cardio. Stay active to keep your energy expenditure high (around 150 minutes of light- to moderate-intensity cardio per week is what I recommend), but don't attempt to "burn off" food, because it likely doesn't work like that anyway and can backfire by negatively influencing your recovery.

That doesn’t mean you should limit activity, since there are plenty of other reasons to do cardio, particularly for your heart. Just don’t rely on cardio alone for fat loss. The biggest driver of body composition change still comes from strength training and nutrition, not from piling on extra miles or minutes.

Recovery: Allows Muscle Growth and Keeps You Consistent

Muscle isn’t built during your workouts, it’s built after them. Recovery allows your muscles to heal, adapt, and grow stronger.

I recommend taking about two days off from lifting each week. Rest days prevent overuse, reduce mental burnout, and help you stay consistent. Mentally reframe your recovery days as productive days in your muscle-building journey.

Not training close enough to failure. Why? It means that you’re not adequately working your muscles—which means you’re not going to get any growth.

Not training close enough to failure happens for several reasons:

How you know you're close to true mechanical failure, not just fatigue:

Failure vs. close to failure: Training to failure means that you cannot do another rep without help. Training close to failure (which is all you need to do) would look like the signs above, meaning you’re 1 to 3 reps away from failure.

I recommend body recomposition as a goal for almost everyone. On the surface, it might sound aesthetic or like another weight-loss strategy, but it’s much more than that. When you build muscle, you’re not just changing how you look; you’re improving your metabolic health, bone density, longevity, and even your mental health.

And, for the record: body recomposition can happen even without weight loss. When muscle increases and fat decreases, your body composition improves, even if the number on the scale doesn’t change.

Pure weight loss actually often causes muscle loss along with fat. That’s because calorie deficits drive weight loss, but when calories drop too low, you end up losing both fat and muscle.

Recomposition takes time, precision, and experimentation. You’ll need to find the right calorie level (which may take some experimenting), balance your training, and stay consistent long enough to see results. But if you commit to this process over the course of a year, you’ll not only improve your body composition, but also build skills and awareness you can use for the rest of your life.

Shannon Ritchey, PT, DPT, is the founder and CEO of Evlo Fitness, a science-based program designed to build muscle with less wear-and-tear on the body. As a doctor of physical therapy with over 15 years of experience, she saw firsthand how high-intensity workouts led to chronic pain, fatigue, and hormonal imbalances in women. Determined to shift the fitness paradigm, she created Evlo — a program led exclusively by Doctors of Physical Therapy, emphasizing strength training through Gentle Consistency™.

Through Evlo and her podcast, The Doctor Shannon Show (4.9 stars, Spotify & Apple, 200+ episodes), Dr. Shannon educates women on the science of exercise, debunking fitness myths and promoting sustainable health. Originally from Kansas, she now lives in Austin, Texas, with her husband and two children.

#4919. Diana Ross at Home: 10 Photos of the Pop Diva Off-Duty

As the breakout star of the Supremes and later a solo sensation, Diana Ross proved herself a force to be reckoned with. The Detroit native’s booming career allowed the singer-slash-actor to fashion her own residences in her own singular style: A statement purple sofa, a shag-lined conversation pit, beaded curtains, and a mirrored piano were among the features that might’ve stood out elsewhere, but all appeared seamlessly at home with The Boss. As she declared in her 1979 anthem of independence: “There’s my chair / I put it there / Everything you see / Is with love and care / It’s my house and I live here.”

Through 12 number-one singles with The Supremes, six number-one hits as a solo artist, a Lifetime Achievement Award from the Recording Academy, and a Presidential Medal of Freedom, Ross apparently managed to maintain a sense of normalcy in her domestic life. “My mom is such a mom,” Ross’s daughter, actor Tracee Ellis Ross, said on The Kelly Clarkson Show. “I had a really regular upbringing.” Read on for a roundup of the icon in her offstage moments.

Ross poses on a floral sofa in her Miami suite in this photo, dated March 17, 1970. It was a big year for the songstress: She performed her final concert as lead singer of The Supremes that January, and her debut solo album was released in June.

The singer unwinds on the sofa during an interview.

When these portraits were snapped, Ross was in the middle of a nine-day run of shows at Miami’s Eden Roc hotel—her official debut solo show after a preview run in Framingham, Massachusetts. “Welcome to the ‘Let’s see if Diana Ross can do it by herself club,’” the vocalist said during the performance, journalist Shirley Eder wrote in a March 20, 1970, issue of The Philadelphia Inquirer. Eder also reported that “Diana takes a long time to unwind after a performance… she screens movies in her suite almost nightly.”

A pregnant Ross leans against her reflective piano.

Ross, eight months pregnant with Tracee in this 1972 photo, leans against the mirrored piano in her Beverly Hills home during an interview about her first film role: Billie Holiday in Lady Sings the Blues, directed by Sidney J. Furie. Ross’s depiction of the jazz singer garnered her a Golden Globe and an Oscar nomination, among other accolades. “She’s the greatest improviser I’ve ever known,” Furie told The Hollywood Reporter in 2021. “Some of the main scenes were improvised…. She was the consummate pro and not a diva at all.”

The musician at home with her daughter Rhonda.

Ross lounges in her Beverly Hills home alongside her eldest child, Rhonda, in 1972. The “Ain’t No Mountain High Enough” singer’s career was launched by Motown founder Berry Gordy, and the two shared a relationship that was romantic as well as professional. Rhonda was born in 1971 as a result of that liaison, but Ross married music executive Robert Ellis Silberstein two months into the pregnancy, and he raised Rhonda as his own. The couple welcomed two more daughters together: Tracee, who was born not long after this photo was taken, and Chudney, who was born in 1975.

Ross at home taping an appearance for an ABC special.

In the mid-1970s, The Wiz star was once again photographed on the purple sofa in her Beverly Hills living area, which was complete with a white-shag-lined conversation pit and a mirror-bedecked fireplace. According to a 1972 New York Times profile of the artist, her 6,833-square-foot home had 30-foot-tall ceilings and an Andy Warhol tapestry of Marilyn Monroe. The grounds also hosted a swimming pool and a tennis court.

Ross with daughters Tracee (left) and Rhonda.

The Ross family’s Beverly Hills pad boasted five bedrooms and nine bathrooms. According to The Los Angeles Times, the property was built in 1964 for art collector Donald Factor. Because its walls maximized space for paintings, it was reportedly known in the neighborhood as “the house with no windows.” The conversation pit abutted a window adorned with a beaded curtain, as shown in the background of this photo of Ross with Tracee and Rhonda. Both daughters followed in their mother’s footsteps as performers: Tracee is an actor (who recently won her first Daytime Emmy for the program Solo Traveling with Tracee Ellis Ross), while Rhonda was nominated for a Daytime Emmy for her role in the soap opera Another World.

Ross, flanked by her daughters, cradles infant Chudney.

A newborn Chudney also made an appearance during the mid-’70s shoot, as seen in this snapshot. Ross and Silberstein divorced in 1977, and the singer went on to marry Norwegian businessman Arne Naess Jr. in 1986. With Naess, she welcomed two sons, Ross and Evan. The “I’m Coming Out” singer and her second husband split in 2000.

In 1978, a newly single Ross welcomed Barbara Walters to her Beverly Hills home for an interview about her career and life as a single mother in front of a window overlooking the backyard pool. “Her success has brought her every material comfort,” Walters narrated in the television special. “Believe it or not, within 24 hours before we came here she completely refurnished the living room.” The television special offered a glimpse of the conversation pit, now done up with dark maroon carpet and a navy sofa with white cushions.

Ross dressed to impress for her birthday party.

The “Upside Down” singer celebrated her 38th birthday with a party at her Beverly Hills residence in March 1982, as seen in this glamorous snapshot. Later that year, she released Silk Electric, her 13th studio album, and was honored with a star on the Hollywood Walk of Fame.

Ross’s daughters were in attendance, as shown in this photo of her bustling birthday party. The 13-time Grammy nominee sold the Beverly Hills property three years later for $1.75 million. These days, the 82-year-old icon lives at Quarry Farm, a property in Greenwich, Connecticut. In 2021, she released her 25th studio album, Thank You.

#4915. Andy Baraghani’s New NYC Restaurant is Full of Design Surprises

“I’ve always been obsessed with design, since I was a kid,” says Andy Baraghani. “I love how restaurant interiors can take you to a different place.”

At Ditto—the all-day café he is opening in New York’s Flatiron—the chef, cookbook author, and YouTube star has given that obsession free rein. With each design decision—from the vintage glass-prism Holophane pendants in the front window to the intense blue (tinted to match Benjamin Moore Buckland Blue) of the rear dining area’s Venetian plaster walls—he asked himself: “Would I actually do this in my home?” If the answer was yes, it passed the test.

The interior of Ditto, with Venetian plaster walls in a shade matching Benjamin Moore Buckland Blue, and Model S22 chairs from Galvanitas.

For Andy Baraghani, design is something of a family affair. His husband, Keith Pollock, is a former AD editor and now a West Elm executive. But opening a restaurant was never part of the plan. In fact, when Michael Silber, a restaurant investor behind New York hot spots like Borgo, Penny, and Bistrot Ha, approached him with the idea of a laid-back restaurant open for breakfast, lunch, and dinner, Baraghani admits his first reaction was: “I have no interest.”

It wasn’t for lack of experience. He had started his career in restaurants—from Chez Panisse in Berkeley, where he worked as a teenager, to Estela in New York—before becoming a food editor at Bon Appetit and winning a James Beard Award for his cookbook, The Cook You Want to Be.

But he bonded with Silber over their shared love of greenmarket cooking and decorating deep dives. When it came to Ditto, he says, he wanted the space to convey his personal style. It helped that he and Silber were on the same page. “Andy and I talked about styles ranging from Bauhaus, de Stijl, and classic modernism to 1970s and ’80s aesthetics from places like Italy and France,” Silber says.

They brought in like-minded collaborators: interior designer Eric Wink and architect Alta Indelman. Wink, a close friend of Baraghani who helped with the design of his and Pollock’s home, says the partners told him that New York’s much-missed City Bakery, which was two blocks uptown from Ditto’s 16th Street location, was an inspiration. “They wanted it to feel casual and approachable, with a bit of a New York diner quality,” Wink says.

Holophane pendant lamps hang in the restaurant’s front window.

The space, in a 1905 former factory (its last incarnation was an agnès b. store), is divided into three zones. In the front section, visitors can shop at a market area with hand-picked groceries. A glossy white counter with Bauhaus curves anchors the middle section; this is where customers line up, take a number, and either carry the food out or dine at laminate-topped tables under a trio of oversized custom fabric pendants. The menu, handwritten on a roll of butcher’s paper and updated daily, features dishes like pomegranate spoon salad and what Baraghani calls “the Platonic ideal of granola,” with tahini, maple syrup, and yogurt drizzled with fig-leaf oil.

The third zone, the one painted blue, features an open kitchen and café tables pulled up to a banquette that runs the length of the room. “There’s that good chaos in the middle and the back is a calmer moment,” says Baraghani.

Indelman, a veteran collaborator with New York restaurateurs like Andrew Tarlow and Gabe Stulman, was tasked with giving the space a utilitarian, high-tech ambience. She clad the walls and the open kitchen in patinated stainless steel and preserved industrial elements like structural arches and polished concrete floors.

But it was Baraghani and Silber who took the lead on honing the visual details of the interiors. “We went through 50 possibilities to find the right chair,” Silber says. The one they selected—a 1960s Dutch industrial chair, the Model S22 from Galvanitas—was sourced from Amsterdam Modern in Los Angeles.

Silber also spent hours on eBay bidding on midcentury ceramic bud vases. “They were each under $20 but put them together on a shelf and you have your own little Giorgio Morandi tableau,” he says.

White wall speakers and a French ceramic mask hang above the banquette, tables, and stools upholstered in Maharam’s Stripe It fabric.

The no-nonsense decor also features white wall speakers, created for the space by sound artist Tom Bloxam, and programmed with playlists created by Michael Scanlon of Chandelier Creative, which oversaw Ditto’s branding. But there are quirkier moments too. When it came to choosing fabric for the custom stools, Baraghani insisted on Maharam’s Stripe It, which he felt had a “Baja California” vibe. “We needed that twist,” he says. For his part, Wink specified his favorite stainless-steel toilet for the restrooms—Neo-Metro’s wall-mounted MiniLoo. “I added a custom wood top to soften it a bit,” Wink says.

Meanwhile, a pair of massive midcentury French ceramic faces that hang on a wall opposite the menu of the day are on loan from Baraghani’s own home. “They anchor the space and add a touch of craft,” he says. “I just knew they needed to be here.”

#4913. The Hoka and On Early Black Friday Deals Worth Shopping This Year

A new pair of walking shoes is even better when you can get it on sale.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

If a fresh pair of walking shoes is on your wish list, chances are Hoka and On have both caught your eye. The two brands have earned plenty of fans, and both are offering early Black Friday deals worth taking a look at if your shoe rotation needs a fresh pair (or two!) added to the mix. But which brand should you go with? Ultimately, the best pick for you comes down to how much cushioning and support you want, plus the fit that feels best on your feet.

Both brands make sneakers that can take you from your daily walk to a full day of errands. Hoka is known for its plush cushioning and rocker-shaped soles, while On offers a range of cushioning levels and its signature CloudTec technology. Each has multiple models to choose from, so it’s always worth comparing individual shoes rather than assuming every pair from one brand will feel the same.

The brand’s thick, supportive soles are a draw for walkers who want a soft feeling underfoot. The Clifton 11 is WH’s best overall Hoka walking shoe: Associate Style and Reviews Editor Shannen Zitz says its “supportive yet flexible cushioning” gives her a spring in her step.

Since the Clifton 11 was just released earlier this year, now’s the time to pick up a pair of last year’s model, the Clifton 10, at a 35 percent discount. Editors loved this shoe just as much.

For longer walks, consider the Arahi 8, which offers a firmer feel and stability. Fitness editor Cori Ritchey praises its “well-balanced cushioning,” noting that it feels plush without compromising her stability on long walks or runs.

The Cloudrunner 3 offers a medium level of cushioning and is WH’s best overall On walking shoe. Executive Health and Fitness Director Jacqueline Andriakos found it “firm and supportive (and not overly cushioned).”

For travel and casual walks, the lightweight Cloud 6 is a breathable, easy-on option. The trade-off: It offers less arch and ankle support than a more structured shoe, so it may not be the best fit if stability is a priority.

Whether you’re leaning toward Hoka’s plush cushioning or On’s range of supportive styles, the best deal is on a pair that feels good on your feet. We’ll keep this story updated with the latest brand sales, so check back as you shop.

#4910. Gwyneth Paltrow Is Launching a New Podcast—Take the First Look Inside the “Goop Guide” Studio

Viewers tuning in to Gwyneth Paltrow’s new podcast, The Goop Guide, will be forgiven for assuming the show was filmed in a room inside her serene and stylish Montecito home. The setting for her new venture (launching on Netflix and Spotify November 11) upends the paradigm of a podcaster on a sofa next to a potted fern.

The Goop Guide is indeed recorded inside a studio, but Paltrow approached the space with just as much intention as her own home, enlisting interior designer Marie Carson to bring it to life. “I wanted it to feel like a beautiful, elevated nook of somebody’s old stone house somewhere,” Paltrow tells AD of the space.

“If we can’t be recording in my home, I want to give guests the feeling of comfort and coziness,” Paltrow says of her goal for the studio’s design.

The catch? Carson only had mere days to turn the sterile studio into something far cozier and conducive to the wide-ranging conversations Paltrow hoped to conduct during the show, which launches with Zoe Saldana as the first guest. “We started to decorate remotely and I looked at it and thought, This is not the direction,” Paltrow says of the space’s first iteration. “And so I called Marie and said, ‘We’re filming in, like, a week and I need you to come and fix this.’”

It wasn’t the tight timeline that initially gave Carson pause. “At first I said, ‘Oh my gosh, I’ve never done a set! What does that mean?’” recalls the designer. “But when Gwyneth said she wanted it to feel like a beautiful room in her home, I thought, Well, I can do that.” Previously, the two had collaborated on a space at Paltrow’s Brentwood property, which meant there was already a creative shorthand between them—incredibly helpful when they had only days to outfit the space before shooting commenced. “Gwyneth has such a strong direction of what she wants and such excellent taste,” Carson says. “So I was able to take her notes and run with it.”

The focal point for the studio’s design is a late-18th-century Flemish tapestry. “It added life to the space,” says Carson.

The Goop Kitchen founder wanted the focal point of the design to be a late-18th-century Flemish tapestry, whose landscape imagery features a palette of muted greens and taupes. “I thought it would be beautiful because of what they’ve lived through and what they’ve seen,” Paltrow says of tapestries. “I love them because you think about all the life going on in front of these things for hundreds and hundreds of years.” To complement the hanging, Carson added a 19th-century Belgian trestle table from Meg Turner Gallery, antique chairs reupholstered in Loro Piana brushed cashmere, and a handwoven hemp and silk rug sourced from Mansour. “Marie’s execution is flawless,” Paltrow says. “She has a real knack for finding the perfect thing that has the right dimensions and warmth.”

Paltrow commissioned a painting of magnolia from artist Rob Reynolds. The sconces are from Goop’s collection with CB2.

A close-up of one of the podcast chairs: an antique sourced from a shop in Pasadena and reupholstered with Loro Piana cashmere.

In the handful of shows Paltrow’s filmed so far, it appears her guests (which include Rick Rubin, Ke Huy Quan, and more) feel similarly at home. “Everyone who walks in is like, ‘I’ve done so many podcasts and I’ve never seen one like this! It feels like a real room and I feel so at ease.’’’ she says. Considering Paltrow will be filming dozens of episodes inside the space, she’s happy it’s comfortable for her as well. “I would definitely live in this room,” she says. “I think about this room all the time now when I’m not in it because I’m so obsessed with it.”

#4904. 15 Best Doormats That Are Durable and Design-Forward (2026)

Displaying a welcoming doormats at your home’s threshold is a crucial opportunity to make a strong first impression on visitors. But it’s not just about wooing your guests upon arrival—they should also have some staying power against the elements and an onslaught of muddy boots this winter.

Thankfully, we found plenty of heavy-duty, high-quality doormats that are both handsome and durable. Many of these options feature natural fibers made from coir, which is mildew-resistant, absorbent, and can survive any kind of weather conditions and foot traffic it endures. Eco-friendly jute is another natural material common among doormat designs—favored for its organic look and its ability to trap dirt while still looking clean. Our top picks include doormats we’ve tested in our own homes as well as a few exciting options from brands we trust, so we don’t suggest (figuratively) dragging your feet on these great designs.

Ruggable’s entire door mat catalog is machine washable, which feels like a non-negotiable for an item literally made to trap dirt. Senior commerce editor Nashia Baker found a kindred match in this pick, telling us that “the balance of whimsical lines and shapes makes walking through the door a harmonious experience.” She also noted that it’s “incredibly durable,” and after months of high-traffic it “can handle any wet or muddy shoes that land on it without getting waterlogged.” Non-slip backing keeps it (and you) from sliding around.

Lean into a modern nautical vibe (think: this contemporary Long Island escape) with Serena & Lily’s chunky mat that looks like it was woven from stolen sailboat rope. The natural/coastal blue colorway offers just a hint of color, while monochromatic options that weave in multiple shades of blue and white make a more vibrant statement.

Sometimes all you need to make a statement is a simple pattern. This honeycomb design is subtle, mildew-resistant, shed-free, and fade-safe. A rubber backing means there won’t be any slipping and it’s designed to hold water—the raised border design helps contain up to 1.5 gallons of water per square yard.

Quilt-like lines feed into a larger geometric pattern in this doormat design that still feels simple but very modern. The grid of black rectangular lines reads as a neutral, and the elongated shape covers more area than some of its peers on this list, so we’d recommend it for an entry hallway or wider doorsteps.

This Heymat doormat is a durable option for covered porches and entryways. Not suitable for true outdoor use (on an uncovered patio or anywhere susceptible to the elements), this Heymat doormat is highly absorbent and soft underfoot. Shopping market editor Kate McGregor’s family owns a few mats from this brand, which they use as an oversized entryway mat in the mudroom for muddy boots and dog paws, as well as a bathmat in the guest bathroom. “This multi-colored mat has held up so well against summer mud and dropped birdseed, despite its pastel grid design,” she says. “Even after five years, the colors have stayed vibrant, and the mat never gets soggy.” Plus, whether sprayed down or shaken, she notes that the cleanup is very easy. “I might protest if my mom ever decides to replace it.”

A departure from the traditional rectangular doormat shape, this Toast alternative offers colorful texture in one compact oval shape that’s fully reversible. This one is crafted from woven and braided jute strands that are ideal for trapping loose dirt and debris. For those looking for an alternative to standard brown jute styles, these alternating patterns are pretty mesmerizing.

If you’ve got more floor space to fill in your greeting room, smaller sizes of The Citizenry’s jute rugs could be exactly what you’ve been looking for. At 24” x 36,” this hand braided mat has a refined look thanks to the contrasting border design, and it’ll be a nice surface to station wet or damp shoes so you’re not damaging the floor.

In our doormat quest, we also found this woven gem from Matilda Goad’s MG&Co. with looped fringing that looks like scalloped edges. The deep burgundy tone will help hide visible mud stains (though the jute weave can also be easily hosed down when it’s time for a clean). It’s also available in a longer, thinner silhouette to help cover sprawling patios.

Floral without feeling overly whimsical (no shame if that’s your vibe, though), Anthropologie’s sweet, embossed doormat is a simple option for anyone with an already-decorated entryway. With an abundance of five-star ratings, lots of reviews praise the design's versatile look and durable material makeup.

The raised, slatted design of Crate & Barrel’s unfinished teakwood doormat keeps moisture, grime, and dirt away sans-bristles. It’s also great for indoor use, perhaps as a minimalist bath mat. Be mindful that teak patinas over time due to weather and regular wear and tear, shifting from a golden wood to a grayish color.

The bold tulip motif on this doormat has a cheerful Scandi-folk style that can brighten your front entry even in the dead of winter. When it starts to look a little worse for wear, the mat easily lifts off its non-slip base for machine washing. In a spot that receives near-constant foot traffic, the ability to refresh it from season to season will be a godsend.

Danish brand HAY is known for their lively color combinations, and their doormats are no exception. With interlocking strands of navy and pink rope, bookended by charming red trim, this pick welcomes guests in with a jolt of maximalist energy. The vegetable fiber strands offer a strong and durable natural alternative to plastic: Shake it out and spray it down with water to clean it and keep the colors fresh.

A stylish coir braid gives this under-$30 doormat a higher-end look. The thick weave creates an almost padded texture, designed to expertly track dirt as your shoe treads catch on the material. With its lattice-like appearance, we envision this doormat at the front door of your Cotswolds-inspired farmhouse, or perhaps a coastal home that could use another natural texture to contrast the white and blue color palette.

Leave it to Sarah Sherman Samuel to create a great doormat. The AD100 designer, arguably most known for Mandy Moore’s Altadena home, offers a twist on the traditional rectangle. Uneven stripes with cutouts give your entryway a modern edge. The coir is dependable, and the black accents help to disguise the inevitable mess.

CB2’s doormat selection is predominantly composed of versatile Chilewich designs—like this monochromatic pattern that feels like a fashionable take on television static. Designed with functionality first, the shag mat uses antimicrobial yarn to keep itself tidy. It also has a no-slip backing to keep it fixed near your doorstep, even when your shoes need a serious wipe down.

What should you look for in the best outdoor doormats?

Although design is important, you also want to make sure that your doormat is low profile enough to fit under your door and also has a non-slip backing to keep your guests from busting it before they make it into your home. (Talk about a first impression!) Materials like PVC with rubber backing are key for the latter, while 100% coir doormats give your entryway a more natural look and are more ideal for trapping dirt and debris.

The answer is a tentative yes. You’re always going to want to see what the manufacturer recommends and it, of course, depends on where you live. Doormats are always made for outdoor use, but various climates will wear down the materials differently. Frequent rain and snow might soften jute while constant sunshine will fade most textiles. An easy solution, if you aren’t sure what your mat can withstand, is to drag your doormat into the mudroom or somewhere with a bit of shelter whenever there’s big weather on its way.
